follows automatically, and $S_n=2K$ is the unique nonnegative even
integer in $[Q_n,Q_n+2)$. In particular, $K$ is determined by the
compensated observation without knowing $S_n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 \Phi_r(p)=-\frac{3r^2}{p}+\frac{r^3}{p^2}.
\]
For positive $p,r$, direct algebra gives the exact identity
\[
 \Phi_r(p)-\Phi_r(r)
   =(p-r)-\frac{(p-r)^3}{p^2}.
\]
By the definition of the finite moments,
$Q_n=\sum_{i=0}^n\Phi_r(p_{\sigma(i)})
          -\sum_{i=0}^n\Phi_r(p_i)$.
Cancel the common coordinates and insert the common reference
$\Phi_r(r)$. Equal cardinalities cancel its constant contribution.
The analogous reference decomposition of $S_n$ therefore gives
\[
 S_n-Q_n=
 \sum_{i\in E_n}\frac{(p_i-r)^3}{p_i^2}
 +\sum_{i\in L_n}\frac{(r-p_i)^3}{p_i^2}.
\]
This retains the compensation and the unit coefficient of $S_n$;
both displayed remainders are nonnegative.

The proved one-probe localization gives
$0\le p_i-r\le4C$ for $i\in E_n$ and
$0\le r-p_i\le C$ for $i\in L_n$.
On the entering set, $p_i\ge r$. On the leaving set,
$p_i\ge r-C>3r/4$. Hence, on either set, the displacement divided
by $p_i$ is at most $4C/r$. It follows that
\[
\begin{split}
 S_n-Q_n
 &\le\frac{16C^2}{r^2}
    \left(\sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)
          +\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)\right)\\
 &=\frac{16C^2}{r^2}S_n
 \le\frac{64C^3}{r^2},
\end{split}
\]
using the maintained bound $S_n\le4C$.

For the readout statement, if $C\ge r/4$ then
$32C^3\ge r^3/2\ge3r^2/2>r^2$, a contradiction.
Thus $r-C>3r/4\ge9/4>2$. Localization excludes the exceptional
prime $2$ from both transported sets, but retains it in the original
prefixes. Their equal cardinalities force $S_n=2K$, $K\ge0$.
The error bound and the precision hypothesis give
$0\le2K-Q_n<2$. An interval of the form $[Q_n,Q_n+2)$ cannot
contain two distinct even integers. This proves the asserted recovery.
\end{proof}

For growing budgets, $C_n^3/p_n^2\to0$ gives eventual exact recovery
with error tending to zero. In particular, the sufficient scale is now
$C_n=o(p_n^{2/3})$, compared with $o(p_n^{1/2})$ for the preceding
single-probe readout. This is a stronger observation theorem.
It supplies no new arithmetic upper bound for the probe cost itself,
so it changes no RH boundary.

\begin{flushleft}
The exact scalar identity and signed entering/leaving comparisons are
compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/CubicReciprocalTransport.lean}.
The two-moment identity, complete signed comparison, cubic error and
unique finite even readout are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileCubicReadout.lean}.
The growing-budget statement is a mathematical consequence of the
finite certificates, rather than a separately named limit declaration.
The coefficient cancellation is elementary; no literature-priority claim
is made for this quantitative application to prime-profile transport.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Geometric compensation and stable logarithmic-moment readout}

For an integer $m\ge0$, define the centered reciprocal polynomial
\[
 \Psi_{m,r}(p)=r\sum_{j=1}^m(1-r/p)^j,\qquad
 P_{m,n}=\sum_{i=0}^n
       \bigl(\Psi_{m,r}(p_{\sigma(i)})-\Psi_{m,r}(p_i)\bigr),
 \quad r=p_n.
\]
The empty sum is zero. Equivalently,
\[
 P_{m,n}=r\sum_{j=1}^m M_{j,n},\qquad
 M_{j,n}=\sum_{i=0}^n
    \bigl((1-r/p_{\sigma(i)})^j-(1-r/p_i)^j\bigr).
\]
These are finite centered power-sum differences of the probabilities.
Binomial expansion expresses $M_{j,n}$ using their ordinary power sums
of degrees at most $j$; the constant terms cancel.
For $m=2$, the polynomial is $\Phi_r(p)+2r$, so $P_{2,n}=Q_n$.

\begin{theorem}[All even orders preserve the exact transport]
Suppose one positive probe satisfies $D_n(t)\le C$, with
$0\le C<r/4$. For every even integer $m=2k\ge0$,
\[
 0\le S_n-P_{m,n}\le4C(4C/r)^m.
\]
If $r\ge3$ and $4C(4C/r)^m<2$, the exact transport $S_n=2K$
is the unique nonnegative even integer in $[P_{m,n},P_{m,n}+2)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The finite geometric identity gives, for every $p>0$,
\[
 \Psi_{m,r}(p)=(p-r)-\frac{(p-r)^{m+1}}{p^m},
 \qquad \Psi_{m,r}(r)=0.
\]
One may verify the identity by induction on $m$; the successor adds
$r(1-r/p)^{m+1}$ and cancels the preceding remainder.
After the same common-coordinate cancellation and equal-cardinality
reference decomposition as before, evenness of $m$ gives exactly
\[
 S_n-P_{m,n}=
 \sum_{i\in E_n}\frac{(p_i-r)^{m+1}}{p_i^m}
 +\sum_{i\in L_n}\frac{(r-p_i)^{m+1}}{p_i^m}.
\]
Every summand is nonnegative. The proved localization implies that
the displacement divided by $p_i$ is at most $4C/r$ on both sets.
Each summand is therefore at most $(4C/r)^m$ times its displacement.
Their displacement sum is $S_n\le4C$, proving the bound.
This argument includes $m=0$: both sides reduce to the existing
transport bound. For the readout statement, $r-C>2$ excludes
the exceptional prime from the transported sets, and the error
interval of width less than two identifies the even integer uniquely.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stable recovery from logarithmically many centered moments]
Suppose $r=p_n\ge3$ and one positive probe satisfies $D_n(t)\le r/8$.
Put
\[
 k=\lfloor\log_4 r\rfloor+1,\qquad m=2k.
\]
There is an integer $K\ge0$ with $S_n=2K$ such that, simultaneously
for every real observation $y$,
\[
 |y-P_{m,n}|<\tfrac12
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad |y-2K|<1.
\]
For each such $y$, $K$ is the unique nonnegative integer $j$
with $|y-2j|<1$. Thus nearest-even rounding recovers the exact
transport despite an observation error of almost one half.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since $r<4^k$, the preceding signed estimate with $C=r/8$ gives
\[
 0\le S_n-P_{m,n}
 \le \frac r2(1/2)^{2k}
 =\frac{r}{2\cdot4^k}<\frac12.
\]
The localization window satisfies $r-C=7r/8\ge21/8>2$, so
$S_n=2K$ for some $K\ge0$.
If $|y-P_{m,n}|<1/2$, the retained one-sided error gives
$-1<y-2K<1/2$, hence $|y-2K|<1$.
Two distinct even integers cannot both be at distance less than one
from $y$, proving uniqueness with the same $K$ for every observation.
\end{proof}

The number of centered moments is at most $\log_2 r+2$.
The noiseless readout needs only
$m=2\lfloor\log_4 r\rfloor$, using the interval of width two;
the two extra moments above leave a fixed observation-error allowance.
For example, if each centered difference $M_{j,n}$ is supplied with
absolute error at most $1/(4rm)$, the combined observation
$y=r\sum_{j=1}^m\widehat M_{j,n}$ has error at most $1/4$,
so the stable readout applies. This last statement follows by the
finite sum inequality. Its precision concerns the centered differences;
expanding them into raw powers requires retaining the binomial
coefficient amplification.

\begin{flushleft}
The finite geometric identity, reference value and arbitrary-order
remainder are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalMomentCore.lean}.
The signed coordinate comparisons are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalMomentTransport.lean}.
The complete signed estimate, finite readout, explicit degree
$2\,\operatorname{Nat.log}_4(p_n)$ and uniform noisy readout with degree
$2(\operatorname{Nat.log}_4(p_n)+1)$ are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileMomentReadout.lean}.
Here the natural-number logarithm is the integer base-four logarithm.
The real-logarithm moment-count inequality and the per-moment
measurement-error consequence are ordinary mathematical consequences,
not separate named Lean declarations.
The error theorem retains $r=2$; the stated even-integer recovery
certificates explicitly require $r\ge3$.
The construction improves finite observation accuracy under an
assumed arithmetic cost bound, and leaves the certified RH boundary unchanged.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Uniform recovery on the full localization window}

The actual coordinate denominators sharpen the preceding geometric
estimate. No separation from the endpoint $C=r/4$ is needed.

\begin{theorem}[Full-window signed accuracy and stable readout]
Suppose one positive probe satisfies $D_n(t)\le C$, where
$0\le C<r/4$ and $r=p_n$. For every even order $m=2k\ge0$,
\[
 0\le S_n-P_{m,n}\le 2^{-m}S_n\le4C\,2^{-m}.
\]
If $r\ge3$, put
\[
 k=\lfloor\log_4(2r)\rfloor+1,\qquad m=2k.
\]
Then there is one integer $K\ge0$, with $S_n=2K$, such that every
observation $y$ satisfying $|y-P_{m,n}|<1/2$ has $2K$ as its
unique nonnegative even integer at distance less than one.
The degree is independent of $C$ throughout $0\le C<r/4$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For an entering coordinate $q$, localization gives
$q-r\le4C<r$, hence $q<2r$ and
\[
 \frac{q-r}{q}<\frac12.
\]
For a leaving coordinate $p$, it gives
$r-p\le C<r/4$, hence $p>3r/4$ and
\[
 \frac{r-p}{p}<\frac13<\frac12.
\]
The exact even-order remainder from the preceding theorem therefore
satisfies, with its two signs retained,
\[
\begin{split}
 S_n-P_{m,n}
 &=\sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)
       \left(\frac{p_i-r}{p_i}\right)^m
   +\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)
       \left(\frac{r-p_i}{p_i}\right)^m\\
 &\le2^{-m}
    \left(\sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)+\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)\right)
 =2^{-m}S_n.
\end{split}
\]
The maintained bound $S_n\le4C$ completes the uniform error estimate,
including $m=0$.

For the chosen degree, $2r<4^k$, so
\[
 0\le S_n-P_{m,n}\le\frac{4C}{4^k}
   <\frac r{4^k}<\frac12.
\]
Since $r-C>2$, the same exceptional-prime argument gives $S_n=2K$.
Combining the retained one-sided error with
$|y-P_{m,n}|<1/2$ yields $-1<y-2K<1/2$.
Thus $|y-2K|<1$, and distinct even integers again prove uniqueness
with the same $K$ before all possible observations are chosen.
\end{proof}

The degree is at most $\log_2 r+3$. This covers every budget in the
full localization window, including budgets approaching its upper
endpoint, with no deterioration of the moment count or the observation
noise allowance. The previously stated per-centered-moment accuracy
condition applies with this new value of $m$.

\begin{flushleft}
The arbitrary relative-ratio remainder and the two half-window
coordinate comparisons are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalMomentWindow.lean}.
The complete signed sandwich, retained $2^{-m}S_n$ error, and uniform
noisy readout are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileFullWindowReadout.lean}.
The formal degree uses $\operatorname{Nat.log}_4(2p_n)$;
the real-logarithm upper bound on the degree is an ordinary consequence.
As before, the error estimate includes the first prime and the
even-integer readout explicitly assumes $p_n\ge3$.
This is a stronger observation certificate under the same localization
hypothesis; it supplies no new arithmetic estimate of that hypothesis.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Polynomial square filters preserve the full transport}

The geometric polynomial is one choice in a larger class of finite
probability observables. This gives freedom to suppress the complete
remainder while retaining the principal transport coefficient exactly.

\begin{theorem}[Arbitrary normalized polynomial filters]
Let $H\in\mathbb R[X]$ satisfy $H(0)=1$, and suppose
\[
 |H(x)|\le\eta\qquad(1/2\le x\le4/3).
\]
There is a polynomial $G$ with $\deg G\le2\deg H$ such that, for every
$p>0$,
\[
 \Phi_{H,r}(p):=(p-r)\bigl(1-H(r/p)^2\bigr)=rG(r/p).
\]
Define the complete signed observable
\[
 A_{H,n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\Phi_{H,r}(p_{e(i)})
              -\sum_{i=0}^{n}\Phi_{H,r}(p_i),\qquad r=p_n.
\]
Under the same hypothesis $D_n(t)\le C$ at one positive probe,
with $0\le C<r/4$,
\[
 0\le S_n-A_{H,n}\le\eta^2S_n\le4C\eta^2.
\]
Thus $A_{H,n}$ is a finite linear combination of probability moments
of orders at most $2\deg H$, with principal coefficient one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $R=1-H^2$. Since $R(0)=0$, write $R=XQ$ and set
$G=(1-X)Q$. Then $\deg G\le2\deg H$; the zero polynomial case
is immediate. For $p\ne0$,
\[
 rG(r/p)=r(1-r/p)Q(r/p)
        =(p-r)R(r/p)=\Phi_{H,r}(p).
\]
The constant coefficient of $G$ cancels between the two equal-size
prefixes, so this is genuinely a finite probability-moment observable.

Cancel the common coordinates before estimating anything. On $E_n$,
localization gives $r\le p_i<2r$; on $L_n$, it gives
$3r/4<p_i\le r$. Consequently $r/p_i\in[1/2,4/3]$
on both retained supports. No such assertion is needed for a common
small-prime coordinate. Equal cardinalities give the exact signed identity
\[
\begin{split}
 S_n-A_{H,n}
 &=\sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)H(r/p_i)^2\\
 &\quad+\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)H(r/p_i)^2.
\end{split}
\]
Every displayed displacement is nonnegative. Applying the filter bound
to this complete remainder gives
\[
 0\le S_n-A_{H,n}
 \le\eta^2\left(\sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)
                +\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)\right)
 =\eta^2S_n.
\]
Finally use the already proved $S_n\le4C$.
The principal term has not been discarded or bounded separately.
\end{proof}

\begin{flushleft}
The polynomial realization and degree bound are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/PolynomialReciprocalTransport.lean}.
The actual profile polynomial representation, complete signed comparison
and retained $\eta^2S_n$ error are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileSquareFilter.lean}.
These statements allow the identity profile and finite permutations;
the recovered transport may be zero.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{An accelerated quadratic filter and fewer moments}

Consider the explicit polynomial
\[
 B(x)=1-\frac{528}{217}x+\frac{288}{217}x^2
     =\frac{2(12x-11)^2-25}{217}.
\]
It satisfies $B(0)=1$. This is the normalized shifted quadratic
Chebyshev polynomial; the construction below uses its explicit formula.

\begin{theorem}[Accelerated stable moment readout]
Set $H_k=B^k$ and $A_{k,n}=A_{H_k,n}$, for each integer $k\ge0$.
Under $D_n(t)\le C$ at one positive probe, $0\le C<r/4$,
the observable uses probability moments of degree at most $4k$, and
\[
 0\le S_n-A_{k,n}
 \le\left(\frac{25}{217}\right)^{2k}S_n
 \le64^{-k}S_n\le4C\,64^{-k}.
\]
If $r\ge3$, choose
\[
 k=\lfloor\log_{64}(2r)\rfloor+1.
\]
There is a single $K\ge0$ with $S_n=2K$ such that every observation
$y$ with $|y-A_{k,n}|<1/2$ satisfies $|y-2K|<1$, and no other
nonnegative even integer has distance less than one from $y$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $1/2\le x\le4/3$, the quantity $12x-11$ lies in $[-5,5]$.
Hence
\[
 -25\le2(12x-11)^2-25\le25,\qquad
 |B(x)|\le\frac{25}{217}.
\]
It follows that $H_k(0)=1$, $\deg H_k\le2k$, and
$|H_k(x)|\le(25/217)^k$ throughout the retained window.
The preceding theorem therefore gives the exact-rate error.
The rational inequality $(25/217)^2<1/64$ gives the stated
convenient geometric bound, also valid at $k=0$.

For the specified $k$, we have $2r<64^k$, and consequently
\[
 0\le S_n-A_{k,n}\le\frac{4C}{64^k}
       <\frac r{64^k}<\frac12.
\]
The localization inequality $r-C>2$ excludes the prime $2$ from
the transported coordinates. Equal entering and leaving cardinalities
then imply $S_n=2K$ for some $K\ge0$, as in the earlier parity proof.
Combining the one-sided error with $|y-A_{k,n}|<1/2$ gives
$-1<y-2K<1/2$. Distinct even integers are separated by two,
which proves uniqueness uniformly over all observations.
\end{proof}

At the same degree $m=4k$, the previous geometric certificate gave
$2^{-m}S_n=16^{-k}S_n$; the quadratic filter gives $64^{-k}S_n$.
Thus the certified remainder is smaller by a factor $4^{-k}$.
The explicit degree is at most
\[
 4\bigl(\lfloor\log_{64}(2r)\rfloor+1\bigr)
 \le \frac23\log_2 r+\frac{14}{3},
\]
compared with the earlier $\log_2r+3$ bound. The leading coefficient
in the logarithmic moment count is reduced by one third.
This real-logarithm comparison is an ordinary consequence of the
compiled integer-logarithm certificate; it is not a separate Lean theorem.

The fixed noise allowance concerns the combined observable.
It does not assert that all raw probability moments can be measured
with the same accuracy. If $G(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}a_jx^j$ and the measured
raw moment differences have errors at most $\epsilon_j$, their induced
observable error is bounded by
\[
 r\sum_{j=1}^{m}|a_j|r^j\epsilon_j.
\]
Requiring this quantity to be less than $1/2$ suffices.
The constant moment cancels exactly.

\begin{flushleft}
The explicit quadratic normalization, degree and all-power window bounds
are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/QuadraticReciprocalFilter.lean}.
The degree-$4k$ profile representation, full $64^{-k}S_n$ comparison,
and explicit noisy even-integer recovery are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileQuadraticFilterReadout.lean}.
The sharper $(25/217)^{2k}$ error is a consequence of the compiled
general filter theorem and compiled norm bound.
The error statements retain the first prime; the even-integer readout
assumes $p_n\ge3$ explicitly. No minimax optimality is asserted.
This improves observation accuracy under the same arithmetic cost
hypothesis. It does not establish a stronger bound on that cost or
change the certified RH boundary.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{All-order filters and a halved logarithmic moment count}

The quadratic can be replaced by a polynomial of every order.
The required uniform bound and nonzero denominator follow from an
elementary coupled recurrence; no trigonometric continuation is needed.

Define polynomials $T_k,U_k$ by
\[
 T_0=1,\quad U_0=0,\qquad
 \begin{cases}
 T_{k+1}=XT_k-(1-X^2)U_k,\\
 U_{k+1}=T_k+XU_k.
 \end{cases}
\]
Here $U_k$ is the shifted second-kind coordinate, so the initial
zero polynomial is part of the definition.
Induction gives $\deg T_k\le k$ and $\deg U_k\le k-1$ for $k\ge1$,
with $U_0=0$ handled separately.

\begin{lemma}[Unit-window invariant and positive normalizer]
For every $k\ge0$ and real $x$,
\[
 T_k(x)^2+(1-x^2)U_k(x)^2=1.
\]
Consequently $|T_k(x)|\le1$ on $[-1,1]$.
At the exterior point $11/5$, the normalizer
$a_k=T_k(11/5)$ satisfies
\[
 a_k>0,\qquad 4^k\le2a_k.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Writing $d=1-x^2$, the recursion preserves the quadratic form because
\[
 (xT-dU)^2+d(T+xU)^2=T^2+dU^2.
\]
The initial value is one. For $|x|\le1$ its second term is
nonnegative, so $T_k(x)^2\le1$, including both endpoints.

For the exterior estimate, put $b_k=U_k(11/5)$. The exact recursion is
\[
 a_{k+1}=\frac{11}{5}a_k+\frac{96}{25}b_k,\qquad
 b_{k+1}=a_k+\frac{11}{5}b_k.
\]
We retain the coupled inequalities
\[
 b_k\ge0,\qquad a_k\ge\frac95b_k,\qquad
 V_k:=a_k+\frac95b_k\ge4^k.
\]
They hold at $k=0$. Assuming them at $k$, the next $b$ is nonnegative,
and
\[
 a_{k+1}-\frac95b_{k+1}
 =\frac25a_k-\frac3{25}b_k
 \ge\frac35b_k\ge0.
\]
Moreover
\[
 V_{k+1}=4V_k+\frac35b_k\ge4^{k+1}.
\]
Thus all three inequalities hold by induction. Since $V_k\le2a_k$,
we obtain $4^k\le2a_k$ and $a_k>0$.
The normalizing denominator has therefore been controlled before division.
\end{proof}

Define
\[
 H_k(x)=\frac{T_k((11-12x)/5)}{a_k},\qquad
 \mathcal A_{k,n}=A_{H_k,n}.
\]
Then $H_k(0)=1$, $\deg H_k\le k$, and the affine map sends
$[1/2,4/3]$ into $[-1,1]$. The preceding lemma gives
\[
 |H_k(x)|\le\frac1{a_k}\le2\,4^{-k}
 \qquad(1/2\le x\le4/3).
\]

\begin{theorem}[All-order stable readout]
Under the same one-positive-probe hypothesis $D_n(t)\le C$,
where $0\le C<r/4$ and $r=p_n$, the observable $\mathcal A_{k,n}$
uses probability moments of degree at most $2k$, and
\[
 0\le S_n-\mathcal A_{k,n}
    \le4\,16^{-k}S_n\le16C\,16^{-k}
 \qquad(k\ge0).
\]
If $r\ge3$, choose
\[
 k=\lfloor\log_{16}(8r)\rfloor+1.
\]
There is a single $K\ge0$ with $S_n=2K$ such that every observation
$y$ satisfying $|y-\mathcal A_{k,n}|<1/2$ has $2K$ as its unique
nonnegative even integer at distance less than one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply the complete signed square-filter theorem with
$\eta=2\,4^{-k}$. The principal coefficient remains one, and the
actual error is still
\[
 \sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)H_k(r/p_i)^2
 +\sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)H_k(r/p_i)^2.
\]
The squared norm is $4\,16^{-k}$, giving the stated error bound.
The polynomial representation has degree at most $2\deg H_k\le2k$.

For the displayed choice of $k$, $8r<16^k$. Hence
\[
 0\le S_n-\mathcal A_{k,n}\le\frac{16C}{16^k}
       <\frac{4r}{16^k}<\frac12.
\]
As before $r-C>2$, so the retained entering and leaving primes are
odd and equal cardinalities imply $S_n=2K$ with $K\ge0$.
The combined error bounds give $-1<y-2K<1/2$.
Separation of even integers proves uniqueness for every observation
with this same $K$.
\end{proof}

The explicit degree now satisfies
\[
 m\le2\bigl(\lfloor\log_{16}(8r)\rfloor+1\bigr)
   \le\frac12\log_2r+\frac72.
\]
Thus its leading logarithmic coefficient is half the original
full-window geometric certificate's coefficient.
At a matched even degree $m=2k$, the new complete error bound is
$4\,4^{-m}S_n$, compared with $2^{-m}S_n$ previously; it is strictly
smaller for every $m>2$. These real-logarithm and matched-degree
comparisons are ordinary consequences of the compiled formulas.
The observation-noise allowance remains a condition on the combined
observable. The coefficient-weighted raw-moment accuracy requirement
from the preceding subsection still applies.

\begin{flushleft}
The recurrence, degree bounds, quadratic invariant, unit-window norm
and rational growth packet are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalChebyshevCore.lean}.
The positive-denominator normalization, affine degree bound and
uniform filter norm are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalChebyshevFilter.lean}.
The actual probability-moment representation, complete signed error
and explicit integer-logarithm noisy recovery are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileChebyshevReadout.lean}.
All readout certificates are exported together by
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileReadouts.lean}.
The error theorem includes $p_n=2$; even-integer recovery assumes
$p_n\ge3$. Identity profiles and finite permutations remain allowed.
No minimax optimality or external-priority claim is made.
The gain concerns finite observation accuracy under an assumed
arithmetic cost bound, and supplies no new RH boundary.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Exact spectral error and cost-adaptive recovery}

The coupled argument admits an exact refinement. This keeps both
characteristic branches of the normalizer and gives a sufficient recovery
condition in terms of the available cost budget.

For a real exterior point $a\ge1$, choose $0\le c\le a$ with
$c^2\le a^2-1$, and put $\delta=a^2-1-c^2\ge0$.
Writing $A=T_k(a)$ and $B=U_k(a)$, the exact step identities are
\[
\begin{split}
 T_{k+1}(a)-cU_{k+1}(a)
   &=(a-c)(A-cB)+\delta B,\\
 T_{k+1}(a)+cU_{k+1}(a)
   &=(a+c)(A+cB)+\delta B.
\end{split}
\]
They preserve the cone $B\ge0$, $A-cB\ge0$ and imply
$(a+c)^k\le A+cB\le2A$. In particular the exterior normalizer
is positive. On the characteristic boundary $\delta=0$, both
linear forms have exact powers:
\[
 T_k(a)+cU_k(a)=(a+c)^k,\qquad
 T_k(a)-cU_k(a)=(a-c)^k.
\]
Indeed the displayed step identities multiply the two forms by
$a+c$ and $a-c$, respectively, and both start at one.

Take $a=11/5$ and $c=4\sqrt6/5$, and define
\[
 \lambda=\frac{11+4\sqrt6}{5},\qquad
 \mu=\frac{11-4\sqrt6}{5},\qquad
 W_k=\lambda^k+\mu^k.
\]
Here $\lambda>4$, $\mu>0$, and $\lambda\mu=1$, so
\[
 W_k=\lambda^k+\lambda^{-k}
     =2T_k(11/5)>0.
\]
These sign and nonvanishing conditions hold for every $k\ge0$,
including $W_0=2$. The preceding normalized filter therefore satisfies
\[
 |H_k(x)|\le\frac{2}{W_k}
 \qquad(1/2\le x\le4/3).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact spectral certificate]
For the preceding observable $\mathcal A_{k,n}$, suppose
$D_n(t)\le C$ at one positive probe, $0\le C<r/4$, $r=p_n$.
Then, for every $k\ge0$,
\[
 0\le S_n-\mathcal A_{k,n}
   \le\frac{4S_n}{W_k^2}\le\frac{16C}{W_k^2}.
\]
The degree remains at most $2k$.
If $r\ge3$ and
\[
 32C<W_k^2,
\]
there is a single $K\ge0$ with $S_n=2K$ such that every observation
$y$ with $|y-\mathcal A_{k,n}|<1/2$ has $2K$ as its unique
nonnegative even integer at distance less than one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The exact characteristic identities prove the formula for $W_k$.
The numerator of $H_k$ has absolute value at most one on the
retained window, so division by its positive normalizer $W_k/2$
gives the stated filter norm. Apply the same complete signed
comparison, with its unchanged remainder
\[
 \sum_{i\in E_n}(p_i-r)H_k(r/p_i)^2+
 \sum_{i\in L_n}(r-p_i)H_k(r/p_i)^2.
\]
This proves $0\le S_n-\mathcal A_{k,n}\le4S_n/W_k^2$.
The maintained estimate $S_n\le4C$ gives the last inequality.

Under $32C<W_k^2$, positivity of $W_k^2$ gives
$16C/W_k^2<1/2$. The exceptional-prime argument is unchanged:
$r-C>2$, so $S_n=2K$ for some $K\ge0$.
Combining the one-sided error with the observation noise gives
$-1<y-2K<1/2$, and separation of even integers proves uniqueness.
The same $K$ works before any observation is chosen.
\end{proof}

This is a condition on $C$ and the chosen order, rather than a
requirement to use the earlier worst-case order depending only on $r$.
Since $\mu>0$ and $\lambda>4$, we have $W_k>4^k$ for every $k\ge0$.
Thus the exact error coefficient $4/W_k^2$ is strictly smaller than
the previously used $4\,16^{-k}$.
Also $W_k^2\ge\lambda^{2k}$, so a sufficient condition at an even
degree $m=2k$ is
\[
 \lambda^m>32C.
\]
For $C>0$, any nonnegative even $m>\log(32C)/\log\lambda$ suffices.
Uniformly over $C<r/4$, one can instead choose an even degree with
\[
 m>\frac{\log(8r)}{\log\lambda},\qquad
 m\le\frac{\log(8r)}{\log\lambda}+2.
\]
Its leading coefficient relative to $\log_2r$ is
$\log2/\log\lambda<1/2$.
These real-logarithm choices and coefficient comparisons are ordinary
consequences of the compiled exact formula and spectral signs.
No minimum-degree or minimax assertion is made.
The noise condition still concerns the combined observable; raw-moment
measurement errors retain their coefficient weights.

\begin{flushleft}
The quantified exterior cone and both characteristic identities are
compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalChebyshevCore.lean}.
The exact two-branch denominator, reciprocal-root identities, positivity,
and exact filter norm are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/ReciprocalChebyshevFilter.lean}.
The full signed spectral bound and the cost-adaptive noisy readout are
compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileChebyshevReadout.lean}.
The error estimate includes the first prime, whereas even-integer recovery
explicitly assumes $p_n\ge3$. Finite permutations and zero transport remain
included. This proves a stronger finite observation certificate under the
same arithmetic hypotheses, without a new arithmetic cost estimate or
an improved RH boundary.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Nonlinear coercivity and the half-reference window}

The preceding localization used a tangent estimate for each entering
coordinate. Integrating the exact compensated kernel gives a stronger
bound on the whole signed transport. Fix a cutoff $n\ge0$ and put
$r=p_n$, with the prefix containing exactly $n+1$ primes. The probe
$t>0$ is arbitrary and may be chosen independently at each cutoff.
Write
\[
 \begin{split}
 B_t(x)&=\log(1+t/x)-t/x,\\
 N_t(r;p,q)&=\frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2}\bigl(B_t(q)-B_t(p)\bigr),\\
 f_r(d)&=\frac r2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{(r+d)^2}\right).
 \end{split}
\]
In the gauge $f_r$ we require $r>0$ and $d\ge0$.

\begin{theorem}[Full signed nonlinear coercivity]
For every prime-coordinate permutation, every cutoff, and every
positive probe, the complete displacement and normalized cost satisfy
\[
 0\le S_n,\qquad
 f_r(S_n)\le D_n(t).
\]
Consequently, if $D_n(t)\le C<r/2$, then
\[
 S_n\le r\left(\frac1{\sqrt{1-2C/r}}-1\right).
\]
The hypotheses imply $C\ge0$. Every denominator is positive on the
stated domain.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The exact kernel is
\[
 B_t'(x)=\frac{t^2}{x^2(x+t)}\qquad(x>0).
\]
For an entering coordinate $q\ge r$, the function
\[
 F(x)=\frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2}B_t(x)+\frac{r^3}{2x^2}
\]
has derivative
\[
 F'(x)=\frac{r^2t(x-r)}{x^3(x+t)}\ge0
       \qquad(r\le x\le q).
\]
Comparing its endpoint values gives the probe-uniform inequality
\[
 N_t(r;r,q)\ge\frac r2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right)
             =f_r(q-r).
\]
For a leaving coordinate $0<p\le r$, the maintained exact lower
comparison gives
\[
 N_t(r;p,r)\ge r\left(\frac rp-1\right)\ge r-p
             \ge f_r(r-p).
\]
The middle inequality follows from
$r(r/p-1)-(r-p)=(r-p)^2/p\ge0$. For the last inequality, the exact
identity
\[
 d-f_r(d)=\frac{d^2(3r+2d)}{2(r+d)^2}\ge0
\]
holds for every $d\ge0$; also $f_r(d)\ge0$.

The gauge is subadditive. To see this without changing any signed
main term, set
\[
 a=\frac r{r+x},\quad b=\frac r{r+y},\quad
 c=\frac r{r+x+y}\qquad(x,y\ge0).
\]
Then $0\le a,b,c\le1$ and
\[
 c-ab=\frac{rxy}{(r+x)(r+y)(r+x+y)}\ge0.
\]
Hence $c^2\ge a^2b^2$, and
\[
 1-c^2\le1-a^2b^2
  =(1-a^2)+(1-b^2)-(1-a^2)(1-b^2)
  \le(1-a^2)+(1-b^2).
\]
Multiplying by $r/2$ proves
$f_r(x+y)\le f_r(x)+f_r(y)$; induction gives its finite-sum form.

Let
\[
 E_n=e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,n\},\qquad
 L_n=\{0,\ldots,n\}\setminus e(\{0,\ldots,n\}).
\]
They have equal cardinality. Cancelling common coordinates and
retaining the boundary reference gives the exact identities
\[
 \begin{split}
 S_n&=\sum_{j\in E_n}(p_j-r)+\sum_{j\in L_n}(r-p_j),\\
 D_n(t)&=\sum_{j\in E_n}N_t(r;r,p_j)
          +\sum_{j\in L_n}N_t(r;p_j,r).
 \end{split}
\]
Each displacement is nonnegative. Apply finite subadditivity to the
first identity and the scalar bounds to each reference cost in the
second. This proves $0\le f_r(S_n)\le D_n(t)$ and $S_n\ge0$.
No estimate of the magnitudes of the original signed terms is used.

Finally, $f_r(S_n)\le C<r/2$ implies
\[
 0<1-\frac{2C}{r}\le
       \left(\frac r{r+S_n}\right)^2.
\]
Both square roots are nonnegative, and $r+S_n>0$, so
\[
 \sqrt{1-2C/r}\,(r+S_n)\le r.
\]
Division by the strictly positive square root proves the displayed
localization. Nonnegativity of the gauge also gives $C\ge0$.
\end{proof}

The finite-set theorem is more general than the prime specialization:
it applies to any two equal-cardinality finite sets whose retained
coordinates lie on the stated sides of a positive reference.
Only the leaving coordinates need an explicit positivity hypothesis;
the entering coordinates are automatically positive.

This extends the available localization domain from $C<r/4$ to
$C<r/2$. Within the former domain the new upper bound approaches
$r(\sqrt2-1)$ as $C$ increases to $r/4$, whereas the old bound
$S_n\le4C$ approaches $r$. The new theorem includes the first prime
$r=2$, every finite permutation, and zero displacement.
It makes no even-integer readout assertion at the exceptional first
prime and does not assume an eventual prime-ratio estimate.
The boundary prime is $p_n$ throughout, while both finite prefixes
have length $n+1$; there is no shift to $p_{n+1}$ in the cost.

\begin{flushleft}
The exact derivative, integrated reference costs, subadditive gauge,
finite signed assembly and inverse bound are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/CompensatedReciprocalCoercivity.lean}.
The prime-profile coercivity and half-reference localization are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileNonlinearCoercivity.lean}.
The public export is
\path{Assembly/ProbabilityProfiles.lean}.
This is a stronger estimate of the finite probability-profile cost.
It does not estimate the outstanding signed M\"obius packet, and the
certified unconditional RH boundary remains $7/8$.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Rational characteristic acceleration from the integrated window}

The integrated reference costs also sharpen the retained coordinate
window. Keep $D_n(t)\le C$, $0\le C<r/4$, and $r=p_n$.
For each entering prime $q$, its individual reference cost is at most
the full cost, so
\[
 \frac r2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right)\le C<\frac r4,
 \qquad \left(\frac rq\right)^2>\frac12.
\]
For each leaving prime $p$,
\[
 r\left(\frac rp-1\right)\le C<\frac r4,\qquad \frac rp<\frac54.
\]
The entering ratios are at most one, and the leaving ratios are at
least one. Since $(45/64)^2=2025/4096<1/2$, every retained ratio
therefore belongs to
\[
 J=[45/64,5/4].
\]
This assertion concerns precisely $E_n\cup L_n$ after cancellation.
It makes no claim about a common small prime.

The rational left endpoint is chosen to give exact characteristic
roots. The affine map and its exterior parameters are
\[
 z(x)=\frac{125-128x}{35},\qquad
 a=z(0)=\frac{25}{7},\qquad c=\frac{24}{7}.
\]
The map sends $J$ to $[-1,1]$, while $c^2=a^2-1$,
$a+c=7$, and $a-c=1/7$. Define
\[
 V_k=7^k+7^{-k},\qquad
 H_k(x)=\frac{2T_k(z(x))}{V_k}\qquad(k\ge0).
\]
The characteristic identities already proved above give
$V_k=2T_k(25/7)>0$. In particular $H_k(0)=1$ and
$\deg H_k\le k$. The retained-window bound is
\[
 |H_k(x)|\le\frac2{V_k}\le2\,7^{-k}\qquad(x\in J).
\]
Both branches are retained; $V_0=2$ and $H_0=1$.

Use the same signed observable
\[
 A_{k,n}=\operatorname{Def}_{e,n+1}
       \bigl((p-r)(1-H_k(r/p)^2)\bigr),
\]
where $\operatorname{Def}_{e,n+1}$ denotes the permuted finite
prefix sum minus the original finite prefix sum.

\begin{theorem}[Rational characteristic readout]
Under the stated quarter-budget hypotheses, for every $k\ge0$ the
observable $A_{k,n}$ is a probability-moment combination of degree
at most $2k$, and
\[
 \begin{split}
 0\le S_n-A_{k,n}
   &\le\frac{4S_n}{V_k^2}\le\frac{16C}{V_k^2},\\
 S_n-A_{k,n}
   &\le4\,49^{-k}S_n\le16C\,49^{-k}.
 \end{split}
\]
If $r\ge3$ and $32C<V_k^2$, there is a single $K\ge0$ with
$S_n=2K$ such that every observation satisfying
$|y-A_{k,n}|<1/2$ has $2K$ as its unique nonnegative even integer
at distance less than one.
A uniform sufficient integer order is
\[
 k=\left\lfloor\log_{49}(8p_n)\right\rfloor+1.
\]
The Lean statement uses the natural-number logarithm at the positive
integer argument $8p_n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The exact kernel comparisons prove the window as just shown.
The coupled rotation invariant bounds $|T_k(z)|$ by one for
$-1\le z\le1$, and the exterior characteristic identity gives the
strictly positive denominator $V_k$. This proves the stated
polynomial norm and normalization at zero.

The normalized square-filter representation supplies a polynomial
$G_k$ of degree at most $2k$ with
\[
 (p-r)(1-H_k(r/p)^2)=rG_k(r/p).
\]
Thus $A_{k,n}=r\operatorname{Def}_{e,n+1}(G_k(r/p))$.
Only the retained coordinates are used in estimating its error:
\[
 S_n-A_{k,n}=
   \sum_{j\in E_n}(p_j-r)H_k(r/p_j)^2+
   \sum_{j\in L_n}(r-p_j)H_k(r/p_j)^2.
\]
Every displayed displacement is nonnegative, and the principal
coefficient remains one. The support-only comparison therefore gives
$0\le S_n-A_{k,n}\le4S_n/V_k^2$.
The previously compiled full signed estimate $S_n\le4C$ proves
the first cost bound. Since $V_k\ge7^k$, squaring gives
$V_k^2\ge49^k$ and proves both geometric bounds.

For recovery, $r\ge3$ and $C<r/4$ give $r-C>2$.
All retained primes are odd, so the maintained parity theorem gives
$S_n=2K$ for some nonnegative integer $K$.
The condition $32C<V_k^2$ makes the full signed observation error
less than $1/2$. Combining it with $|y-A_{k,n}|<1/2$ puts $y$
at distance less than one from $2K$.
Two different even integers cannot both be at distance less than one
from the same $y$, proving uniqueness with the same $K$ for all
admissible observations.

Finally the chosen natural logarithm gives
$49^k>8r>32C$, hence $V_k^2>32C$. This proves the sufficient
order without a finite sampling or growing-cutoff assumption.
\end{proof}

At matched degree $m=2k$, the geometric error factor changes from
$4\,16^{-k}$ to $4\,49^{-k}$. The explicit sufficient moment degree
has the ordinary real-logarithm bound
\[
 m\le\frac{\log r}{\log7}+\frac{3\log2}{\log7}+2.
\]
Its leading coefficient relative to $\log_2r$ is
$\log2/\log7$, compared with $1/2$ for the earlier geometric
certificate. These degree comparisons are ordinary consequences of
the compiled natural-logarithm choice; no minimum-degree or
minimax claim is made. The exact formula retains the contracting
branch and can require less than this uniform sufficient order.

requires $r\ge3$. Every finite permutation and zero transport remain
included. The noise allowance concerns the combined observable,
with the previously stated coefficient weights on raw moment errors.
This proves a stronger finite observation theorem under the same cost
hypothesis. It supplies no independent bound on the open signed

\subsection{The exact locality threshold for one fixed probe}

The universal half-reference estimate can be complemented by an exact
threshold when the positive probe itself is fixed. Fix $n\ge0$,
$r=p_n$, and $t>0$. Define
\[
 \begin{split}
 c_t(r)&=\frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2},\\
 K_t(r)&=c_t(r)\left(\frac tr-\log(1+t/r)\right).
 \end{split}
\]
The threshold is strictly positive. Throughout this subsection $n$
and $t$ are held fixed while the coordinate permutation varies.

\begin{theorem}[Exact fixed-probe locality threshold]
For every real budget $C$, the following are equivalent:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{There is an integer $M$ such that, for every permutation $e$,}\\
 D_n(t)\le C\ \Longrightarrow\
 e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\subset\{0,\ldots,M-1\};
 \end{gathered}
\]
\[
 C<K_t(r).
\]
More quantitatively, if $D_n(t)\le C<K_t(r)$, every entering prime
$q=p_j$ satisfies
\[
 (K_t(r)-C)\,q(q+t)\le r^2(r+t).
\]
At every budget $C\ge K_t(r)$ the failure is already witnessed by
finite transpositions: for every real $B$ and integer $M$, a swap
of $n$ with some $j>\max(n,M)$ has
\[
 S_n>B,\qquad D_n(t)<K_t(r)\le C.
\]
The same fixed $t$ works for all these witnesses.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $u>0$, the strict logarithmic tangent inequality and its
reciprocal application give
\[
 0<u-\log(1+u)\le\frac{u^2}{1+u}.
\]
Indeed $\log(1+u)<u$. Also
$\log(1/(1+u))\le1/(1+u)-1$, hence
$\log(1+u)\ge u/(1+u)$, which proves the upper bound.
Taking $u=t/r$ proves $K_t(r)>0$.

For any positive coordinate $q$, exact cancellation gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 K_t(r)-N_t(r;r,q)
  &=c_t(r)\left(\frac tq-\log(1+t/q)\right),\\
 0<K_t(r)-N_t(r;r,q)
  &\le\frac{r^2(r+t)}{q(q+t)}.
 \end{split}
\]
This keeps the full compensated logarithmic expression.
For an entering coordinate, the maintained signed reference
decomposition gives $N_t(r;r,q)\le D_n(t)\le C$.
Put $\delta=K_t(r)-C>0$. Then
\[
 \delta\le K_t(r)-N_t(r;r,q)
     \le\frac{r^2(r+t)}{q(q+t)}.
\]
Multiplying by the strictly positive denominator proves the
quantitative coordinate constraint.

Let $A=r^2(r+t)$. Since a prime $q\ge2$ and $t>0$ have
$q+t>1$, the same constraint gives $\delta q\le A$ and thus
$q\le A/\delta$. The zero-based prime enumeration satisfies
$j\le p_j=q$. Choose an integer
\[
 M>\max\left(n+1,\frac A\delta\right).
\]
An image coordinate common to the original prefix has index at most
$n<M$. Every other image coordinate is entering, and its index
satisfies $j\le q\le A/\delta<M$. This proves the finite ambient
prefix assertion, uniformly over all permutations satisfying the
same budget. The argument also covers negative $C$, when applicable
cost constraints may be vacuous.

For the converse, fix any $B\in\mathbb R$ and $M\ge0$.
Choose an integer $Q>B+r$ and put $j=Q+n+M+1$.
Then $j>\max(n,M)$ and $p_j\ge j\ge Q$.
The permutation exchanging exactly $n$ and $j$ has the exact
crossing-prefix identities
\[
 S_n=p_j-r>B,\qquad D_n(t)=N_t(r;r,p_j).
\]
The positive residual already proved gives
$N_t(r;r,p_j)<K_t(r)$. This constructs arbitrarily large
displacement and entering index with the same fixed probe.
If $C\ge K_t(r)$, each such swap satisfies $D_n(t)\le C$.
Its image prefix contains $j>M$, contradicting any proposed
uniform ambient prefix of length $M$.
Thus finite uniform localization implies $C<K_t(r)$.
\end{proof}

The residual estimate also shows, as an ordinary consequence,
that the swap costs approach $K_t(r)$ from below with error at most
$r^2(r+t)/(p_j(p_j+t))$, while their displacements tend to infinity.
The sharpness assertion concerns the budget value for one fixed
probe and cutoff. It does not change the probe with the escaping
coordinate. In the converse, the counterexamples are finite
permutations; no assertion restricted to permutations that are not
finite is used.

This gives an exact criterion for reducing reconstruction from a
single cost inequality to a finite search over possible prefix
images. The complete probability profile and its existing inverse
theorems supply additional information beyond that one inequality.
All statements here include the first prime $r=2$, use a prefix of
length $n+1$, and keep the boundary reference $p_n$.

The strict logarithmic deficit, positive saturation, complete
rational residual and coordinate constraint are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/CompensatedReciprocalSaturation.lean}.
The crossing transposition identity is compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/FinitePrefixTransposition.lean}.
The full-cost incoming bound, uniform ambient-prefix theorem,
unbounded finite-swap witnesses and exact equivalence are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileSaturationThreshold.lean}, exported by
\path{Assembly/ProbabilityProfiles.lean}.
The displayed asymptotic consequence follows from the compiled
residual and unbounded prime enumeration; it is not a separately
compiled limit declaration. No assertion of external priority or
optimal moment degree is made. The outstanding signed arithmetic
estimate and certified RH boundary $7/8$ are unchanged.
\end{flushleft}

\clearpage
\subsection{The sharp locality threshold uniformly over positive probes}

The order of quantifiers changes when a single ambient prefix must
work for every positive probe. The preceding fixed-probe theorem
keeps $t$ fixed; the next theorem requires the same $M$ for all
$t>0$. Again $n\ge0$ is fixed, $r=p_n$, and the observed prefix
has length $n+1$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact probe-uniform locality threshold]
For every real $C$, the following are equivalent:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{There is an integer $M$ such that, for every $t>0$ and permutation $e$,}\\
 D_n(t)\le C\ \Longrightarrow\
 e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\subset\{0,\ldots,M-1\};
 \end{gathered}
\]
\[
 C<\frac r2.
\]
In the subcritical case, every entering prime $q$ satisfies the
probe-independent quadratic constraint
\[
 (r-2C)q^2\le r^3.
\]
At the critical budget itself, finite transpositions already
give unbounded displacement. More precisely, for every real $B$
and integer $M$, choose $j>\max(n,M)$ with $q=p_j$ and
$q-r>B$. The positive probe
\[
 t_{r,q}=\frac{r^3}{2(q^2-r^2)}
\]
and the swap of $n$ with $j$ satisfy
\[
 \begin{split}
 S_n&=q-r>B,\\
 D_n(t_{r,q})&\le\frac r2-\frac{r^3}{4q^2}<\frac r2.
 \end{split}
\]
Thus the failure at every $C\ge r/2$ uses finite permutations
and strictly positive probes, with no limiting probe admitted
as an observation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the upper bound complementary to the maintained
integrated lower bound. For $t>0$ and $q\ge r>0$, put
\[
 c=\frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2},\qquad
 a=\frac{r^2(r+t)}2,\qquad
 F(x)=-cB_t(x)-\frac a{x^2}.
\]
The exact derivative $B_t'(x)=t^2/(x^2(x+t))$ gives
\[
 F'(x)=\frac{r^2(r+t)t}{x^3(x+t)}>0\qquad(x>0).
\]
Consequently $F(r)\le F(q)$, which rearranges to
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_t(r;r,q)
 &\le \frac a{r^2}-\frac a{q^2}\\
 &=\frac{r+t}{2}\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right).
 \end{split}
\]
Together with the integrated lower bound this gives the
two-sided, fully compensated estimate
\[
 \frac r2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right)
 \le N_t(r;r,q)
 \le\frac{r+t}{2}\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right).
\]

Suppose first that $D_n(t)\le C<r/2$.
In the exact signed reference decomposition,
\[
 D_n(t)=\sum_{j\in E}N_t(r;r,p_j)
             +\sum_{i\in L}N_t(r;p_i,r),
\]
the common coordinates have canceled, and every retained reference
cost is nonnegative. Thus an entering $q$ has
$N_t(r;r,q)\le C$. The lower integrated bound yields
\[
 \frac r2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right)\le C,
 \qquad (r-2C)q^2\le r^3.
\]
All denominators multiplied here are positive. Write
$\delta=r-2C>0$. Since $q\ge2$, we have $q\le q^2$, so
$q\le r^3/\delta$. Choose once and for all an integer
\[
 M>\max\left(n+1,\frac{r^3}{r-2C}\right).
\]
A coordinate retained from the original prefix has index at most
$n<M$. Every entering coordinate has
$j\le p_j=q\le r^3/\delta<M$. This $M$ depends only on $n$
and $C$, and therefore works simultaneously for every $t>0$
and every admissible permutation.

For the converse, let $B$ and $M$ be arbitrary. The unbounded
prime enumeration supplies $j>\max(n,M)$ with $q=p_j$ and
$q-r>B$. In particular $q>r$, so $t_{r,q}>0$. Its exact
substitution into the upper bound gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_{t_{r,q}}(r;r,q)
 &\le\frac{r+t_{r,q}}2\left(1-\frac{r^2}{q^2}\right)\\
 &=\frac r2-\frac{r^3}{4q^2}<\frac r2.
 \end{split}
\]
The crossing transposition has exactly this normalized cost and
displacement $q-r$. For $C\ge r/2$ it is budget-admissible,
but its image prefix contains $j>M$. Since $M$ was arbitrary,
no common finite ambient prefix exists. This also treats the
critical equality $C=r/2$.
\end{proof}

The theorem proves that the earlier half-reference condition
for localization is sharp when the positive probe is allowed
to vary. In a fixed-probe experiment the exact threshold remains
$K_t(r)$ from the preceding subsection. Both results concern
the information supplied by one cost-budget inequality;
the complete profile inverse theorem has additional information.
The small prime $r=2$, cutoff $n=0$, and finite permutations
are included without parity restrictions or a rank shift.

\subsection{The complete escape spectrum of a fixed profile probe}

The one-coordinate locality threshold is the first member of a
complete hierarchy. The underlying statement holds for any
increasing sequence with a finite ceiling. This also separates
a finite search for the entire prefix from a finite search for
all but a prescribed number of its coordinates.

Let $w:\mathbb N\to\mathbb R$ be nondecreasing, let $\ell$ be
a finite upper bound, and assume it is approached from below:
\[
 w_i\le\ell\quad(i\ge0),\qquad
 \forall a<\ell\ \exists M:\ a<w_M.
\]
Write
\[
 A_m=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}w_i,\qquad
 \Delta_N(e)=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}w_{e(i)}-A_N,
\]
and, for $1\le k\le N$, define
\[
 T_k=k\ell-(A_N-A_{N-k}).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-prefix escape spectrum]
For every $N\ge1$, $1\le k\le N$, and real $C$, the following
are equivalent:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \exists M\ \forall e:\quad
 \Delta_N(e)\le C\ \Longrightarrow\\
 \#\bigl(e(\{0,\ldots,N-1\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,M-1\}\bigr)<k;
 \end{gathered}
\]
\[
 C<T_k.
\]
The subcritical implication comes from the complete signed bound
\[
 \Delta_N(e)\ge k w_M+A_{N-k}-A_N
\]
whenever at least $k$ image coordinates lie outside the prefix
of length $M$.
For every $C\ge T_k$ and every proposed $M$, a permutation fixed
outside a finite interval has at least $k$ image coordinates
outside that prefix and satisfies $\Delta_N(e)\le T_k\le C$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, any finite set $S\subset\mathbb N$ of cardinality $m$
satisfies
\[
 \sum_{i\in S}w_i\ge A_m.
\]
To see this without discarding signed terms, cancel
$S\cap\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$. The remaining entering and leaving sets
have equal cardinalities. Every entering weight is at least $w_m$,
and every leaving weight is at most $w_m$. Their complete
difference is therefore nonnegative. This is an application of
the maintained signed equal-cardinality reference comparison.

Suppose now that $S=e(\{0,\ldots,N-1\})$ contains at least $k$
indices outside $\{0,\ldots,M-1\}$. Choose exactly $k$ such
indices, forming $U$. The remainder $S\setminus U$ has
cardinality $N-k$, so
\[
 \begin{split}
 \sum_{i\in S}w_i
  &=\sum_{i\in U}w_i+\sum_{i\in S\setminus U}w_i\\
  &\ge k w_M+A_{N-k}.
 \end{split}
\]
Subtracting $A_N$ gives the stated signed bound. In particular,
if $C<T_k$, choose $M$ with
\[
 w_M>\ell-\frac{T_k-C}{k};
\]
the denominator is positive. The ceiling-approximation hypothesis
supplies such an $M$. A budget-admissible prefix with $k$ outside
indices would then have
\[
 \Delta_N(e)\ge T_k-k(\ell-w_M)>C,
\]
a contradiction. The same $M$ works for every admissible
permutation.

For the converse put $a=N-k$ and choose
$b\ge\max(N,M)$. Exchange the two disjoint half-open integer
blocks $[a,a+k)$ and $[b,b+k)$ pointwise, fixing all other indices.
This is an involution and fixes every index at least $b+k$.
Its first $N$ images consist of the unchanged first $N-k$
indices and the complete far block. Thus at least $k$ images
are outside the prefix of length $M$, and its exact cost is
\[
 \begin{split}
 \Delta_N(e)
  &=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w_{b+i}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w_{a+i}\\
  &\le k\ell-(A_N-A_{N-k})=T_k.
 \end{split}
\]
This proves failure at the critical equality as well as at every
larger budget. The entire leaving block remains in the formula.
\end{proof}

For the probability-profile application fix $t>0$ and $n\ge0$,
put $N=n+1$, $r=p_n$, and use the actual scaled weights
\[
 w_i=c_t(r)B_t(p_i),\qquad \ell=0.
\]
They are strictly increasing and strictly negative. The proved
logarithmic deficit bound gives
\[
 0<-B_t(p_i)\le\frac{t^2}{p_i(p_i+t)}.
\]
Since $p_i\ge i$, these weights approach zero from below.
This verifies the ceiling hypothesis on the entire prime
enumeration without a prime number theorem. Moving the fixed
positive factor $c_t(r)$ inside the finite sums gives the exact
identity $\Delta_{n+1}(e)=D_n(t;e)$.

\begin{corollary}[Every fixed-probe escape threshold]
For $1\le k\le n+1$, set
\[
 T_k(t,n)=-c_t(r)\sum_{i=n+1-k}^{n}B_t(p_i).
\]
For every real $C$,
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \exists M\ \forall e:\quad D_n(t;e)\le C\ \Longrightarrow\\
 \#\bigl(e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,M-1\}\bigr)<k
 \end{gathered}
\]
holds if and only if $C<T_k(t,n)$. At $C=T_k(t,n)$,
finite block permutations already put at least $k$ coordinates
beyond every proposed ambient prefix.
The thresholds obey
\[
 \begin{split}
 T_0(t,n)&=0,\qquad T_1(t,n)=K_t(r),\\
 T_{k+1}(t,n)-T_k(t,n)
   &=-c_t(r)B_t(p_{n-k})>0\qquad(0\le k\le n).
 \end{split}
\]
Hence they form a strictly ordered finite spectrum.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply the general theorem to the scaled prime weights just
verified. Canceling the first $n+1-k$ terms of their prefix sum
gives the displayed threshold. The first increment is precisely
$c_t(r)(-B_t(r))=K_t(r)$. Each later increment is the positive
logarithmic deficit of the next earlier prime, with the same
fixed normalizer. These cancellations also give $T_0=0$ and
the exact successor difference.
\end{proof}

Consequently, for $0\le j<n+1$ and a nonnegative budget in
$[T_j,T_{j+1})$, all but at most $j$ coordinates have one common
finite ambient prefix, while $j$ coordinates can escape it in
finite-permutation examples. At a budget at least $T_{n+1}$,
all $n+1$ observed coordinates can be sent beyond every proposed
cutoff. This interval interpretation is a mathematical consequence
of the compiled threshold family. It quantifies precisely how
much positional information one cost inequality retains.
The probe is fixed throughout this spectrum; the preceding
probe-uniform theorem has a different order of quantifiers.
All prime-profile statements include $p_n=2$ and $n=0$, retain
prefix length $n+1$, and require no parity hypothesis.

The interval interpretation is not a separately compiled
combined declaration. No external-priority claim is made.

boundary $7/8$ are unchanged.

\subsection{Simultaneous locality for arbitrary probe collections}

The block witness can be chosen independently of the weight
sequence. This gives an exact statement about simultaneous
budgets, including collections with infinitely many probes.

Let $I$ be any index set. For each $\alpha\in I$, let
$w^{(\alpha)}$ be nondecreasing, bounded above by a finite
$\ell_\alpha$, and cofinal below that ceiling as in the
preceding theorem. At one common prefix length $N$ put
\[
 \begin{split}
 \Delta_{\alpha,N}(e)
   &=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}w^{(\alpha)}_{e(i)}
                        -\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}w^{(\alpha)}_i,\\
 T_{\alpha,k}
   &=k\ell_\alpha-
       \sum_{i=N-k}^{N-1}w^{(\alpha)}_i.
 \end{split}
\]
The budget $C_\alpha$ is any real number for each $\alpha$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact joint-budget locality]
For $N\ge1$ and $1\le k\le N$, the following are equivalent:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \exists M\ \forall e:\quad
 \bigl(\forall\alpha\in I,\
          \Delta_{\alpha,N}(e)\le C_\alpha\bigr)
       \ \Longrightarrow\\
 \#\bigl(e(\{0,\ldots,N-1\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,M-1\}\bigr)<k;
 \end{gathered}
\]
\[
 \exists\alpha\in I:\quad C_\alpha<T_{\alpha,k}.
\]
More strongly, for every $M$ there is one finite permutation $e$
with at least $k$ image coordinates outside that prefix and
\[
 \Delta_{\alpha,N}(e)\le T_{\alpha,k}
       \qquad\text{for every }\alpha\in I.
\]
The same permutation works for the entire index set.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose an index $\alpha$ has $C_\alpha<T_{\alpha,k}$.
Apply the preceding one-weight theorem to obtain a finite
ambient prefix for its budget. Every permutation satisfying
all the budgets satisfies this particular budget, so that
same ambient prefix proves the joint assertion.

Conversely, suppose $C_\alpha\ge T_{\alpha,k}$ for every
$\alpha$. Given any proposed $M$, take $a=N-k$ and
$b=\max(N,M)$, and exchange $[a,a+k)$ with $[b,b+k)$,
fixing every other coordinate. This single permutation has
at least $k$ images outside the prefix of length $M$ and
fixes every coordinate at least $b+k$. For each $\alpha$,
the complete signed block identity gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 \Delta_{\alpha,N}(e)
   &=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w^{(\alpha)}_{b+i}
                   -\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w^{(\alpha)}_{a+i}\\
   &\le k\ell_\alpha-
             \sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w^{(\alpha)}_{a+i}\\
   &=T_{\alpha,k}\le C_\alpha.
 \end{split}
\]
The construction of $e$ uses only $N,k,M$ and is therefore
independent of $\alpha$. It proves all the inequalities
pointwise, so a uniform convergence rate over the index set
is unnecessary. This disproves every proposed joint ambient
prefix. The same calculation proves the stronger common-witness
assertion at the saturation budgets themselves.
\end{proof}

The empty index set is included. In that case the constraints
on the left are vacuous, and the finite block exchange
disproves localization; the existential condition on the
right is false as well.

\begin{corollary}[Arbitrarily many simultaneous positive probes]
Fix $n\ge0$ and $1\le k\le n+1$. Let
$t,C:I\to\mathbb R$ with $t_\alpha>0$ for every $\alpha$.
Then
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \exists M\ \forall e:\quad
 \bigl(\forall\alpha\in I,\
           D_n(t_\alpha;e)\le C_\alpha\bigr)
       \ \Longrightarrow\\
 \#\bigl(e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,M-1\}\bigr)<k
 \end{gathered}
\]
holds if and only if
\[
 \exists\alpha\in I:\quad
 C_\alpha<T_k(t_\alpha,n).
\]
For every proposed $M$, one finite block permutation has at
least $k$ image coordinates outside it while satisfying
$D_n(t_\alpha;e)\le T_k(t_\alpha,n)$ for every $\alpha$
simultaneously.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
 w_i^{(\alpha)}
    =c_{t_\alpha}(p_n)B_{t_\alpha}(p_i),
       \qquad \ell_\alpha=0.
\]
All increasing-weight and ceiling hypotheses were proved in the
preceding specialization for every positive probe. The exact
scaled-cost and threshold identities identify the general
joint theorem with the displayed statement.
\end{proof}

Here a single permutation must satisfy all probe budgets
simultaneously. The earlier probe-uniform theorem asks for a
common ambient prefix for each positive probe's admissible
permutations. These are different quantifiers.
The present theorem classifies the information supplied by
the conjunction of these upper cost bounds on the same
ordered prime coordinates and the same cutoff. Exact profile
values and additional signed arithmetic relations supply
other information. The first prime $2$, the cutoff $n=0$,
and finite permutations remain included.

\subsection{The rational endpoint envelope of all positive probes}

The preceding joint criterion can be evaluated without optimizing a
transcendental function. The exact answer involves two rational sums.
Throughout this subsection the observed prefix is fixed; only the
positive real probe varies.

For $r,p,t>0$ write
\[
 v_t(r,p)=\frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2}
       \left(\frac tp-\log(1+t/p)\right).
\]
For a nonempty finite set $S$ and positive coordinates $(p_i)_{i\in S}$,
put
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 Q_t&=\sum_{i\in S}v_t(r,p_i),&
 A&=\sum_{i\in S}\frac{r^3}{2p_i^2},\\
 B&=\sum_{i\in S}\frac{r^2}{p_i},&
 H&=\sum_{i\in S}\frac{r^3}{2p_i^3}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
In particular $A,B,H>0$ and $m=\#S\ge1$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact rational envelope and explicit probe witnesses]
For every such $S,r,(p_i)$,
\[
 \sup_{t>0}Q_t=\max(A,B).
\]
More precisely, for every $t>0$ and $a\ge1$,
\[
 \begin{split}
 Q_t&\le\frac{r}{r+t}A+\frac{t}{r+t}B
          \le\max(A,B),\\
 Q_t&\ge A-tH,\\
 Q_t&\ge (1-1/a)B-\frac{mr^2a}{t}.
 \end{split}
\]
For every real $C$,
\[
 (\exists t>0:\ C<Q_t)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad C<\max(A,B).
\]
If $C<A$, the explicit choice $t=(A-C)/(2H)$ works.
If $C<B$, put $\delta=B-C>0$; the explicit choices
\[
 a=1+\frac{4B}{\delta},\qquad
 t=\frac{4mr^2a}{\delta}
\]
work. Thus the supremum assertion includes its least-upper-bound
property and actual finite positive witnesses below it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the upper bound with its complete dependence on the
probe. For any real $u$ and $z\ge0$ define
\[
 F_u(z)=(1+u)^2\log(1+z)-(1+2u)z+\frac{z^2}{2}.
\]
Direct differentiation gives
\[
 F_u(0)=0,\qquad F_u'(z)=\frac{(z-u)^2}{1+z}\ge0.
\]
The denominator is positive on the stated domain. Consequently
\[
 (1+2u)z-\frac{z^2}{2}\le(1+u)^2\log(1+z).
\]
Substitute $u=t/r$ and $z=t/p$ and multiply by
$r^4/[t^2(r+t)]>0$. Expanding both sides yields exactly
\[
 v_t(r,p)\le
 \frac{r}{r+t}\frac{r^3}{2p^2}
       +\frac{t}{r+t}\frac{r^2}{p}.
\]
Summation gives the first inequality. Both coefficients are
nonnegative and sum to one, so it also gives the asserted upper
bound by $\max(A,B)$.

For the small-probe lower bound use
\[
 G(z)=z-\log(1+z)-\frac{z^2}{2(1+z)},\qquad
 G'(z)=\frac{z^2}{2(1+z)^2}\ge0,\qquad G(0)=0.
\]
Thus
\[
 v_t(r,p)\ge\frac{r^2(r+t)}{2p(p+t)}.
\]
The exact difference between this rational expression and
$r^3/(2p^2)-tr^3/(2p^3)$ is
\[
 \frac{r^2t(p^2+rt)}{2p^3(p+t)}\ge0.
\]
All denominators are positive. Summing proves $Q_t\ge A-tH$.

For the large-probe lower bound, the logarithmic tangent inequality
$\log x\le x-1$ for $x>0$ implies, for $a\ge1$,
\[
 \begin{split}
 \log(1+t/p)
  &=\log((1+t/p)/a)+\log a\\
  &\le (1+t/p)/a-1+a-1\\
  &\le a+\frac{t}{ap}.
 \end{split}
\]
Here the last step uses $1/a\le1$. The exact identity
\[
 v_t(r,p)=\frac{r^2}{p}-\frac{r^2}{t}\log(1+t/p)
       +\frac{r^3}{t^2}
          \left(\frac tp-\log(1+t/p)\right)
\]
has a nonnegative final term, again by the logarithmic tangent
inequality. Therefore
\[
 v_t(r,p)\ge (1-1/a)\frac{r^2}{p}-\frac{r^2a}{t}.
\]
Summing gives the third estimate.

If $C<A$, the displayed small probe is positive and satisfies
\[
 Q_t\ge A-\frac{A-C}{2}=\frac{A+C}{2}>C.
\]
If $C<B$, the displayed large probe is positive, $a\ge1$, and
\[
 a\delta=\delta+4B,\qquad
 \frac Ba<\frac{\delta}{4},\qquad
 \frac{mr^2a}{t}=\frac{\delta}{4}.
\]
Hence $Q_t>B-\delta/2>C$. Since
$C<\max(A,B)$ means $C<A$ or $C<B$, this proves the witness
implication. Its converse follows from the upper bound.
Finally, an upper bound strictly below $\max(A,B)$ would itself
be exceeded by a positive-probe witness. This proves the exact
least-upper-bound assertion without assuming that a maximizing
finite probe exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact all-positive-probe escape criterion]
Fix $n\ge0$, put $r=p_n$, and let $1\le k\le n+1$. Define
\[
 A_{n,k}=\sum_{i=n+1-k}^{n}\frac{r^3}{2p_i^2},
 \qquad
 B_{n,k}=\sum_{i=n+1-k}^{n}\frac{r^2}{p_i}.
\]
Then
\[
 \sup_{t>0}T_k(t,n)=\max(A_{n,k},B_{n,k}).
\]
For every real $C$, the assertion
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \exists M\ \forall e:\quad
   \bigl(\forall t>0,\ D_n(t;e)\le C\bigr)
      \ \Longrightarrow\\
 \#\bigl(e(\{0,\ldots,n\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,M-1\}\bigr)<k
 \end{gathered}
\]
holds if and only if $C<\max(A_{n,k},B_{n,k})$.
For $k=1$ this cutoff is exactly $C<p_n$.
At or above the cutoff, every proposed $M$ has a finite
block-permutation counterexample satisfying all the probe budgets
simultaneously.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Cancel the initial $n+1-k$ terms in the complete signed threshold:
\[
 T_k(t,n)
   =\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}v_t(r,p_{n+1-k+j}).
\]
The index identity $(n+1-k)+k=n+1$ uses $k\le n+1$.
The endpoint theorem applies to this nonempty finite block,
proving the supremum and the strict-witness criterion.
Apply the preceding joint-budget locality theorem to the
index set of all positive reals, with probe $t$ and constant
budget $C$. Its existential condition is precisely
$\exists t>0:\ C<T_k(t,n)$, which the endpoint theorem has
evaluated.

When $k=1$, the block consists of $p_n=r$ alone, giving
$A_{n,1}=r/2$ and $B_{n,1}=r$. Since $r>0$, their maximum is
$r$. If $C$ is at least the maximum, every individual threshold
is at most $C$, so the common finite block witness from the joint
theorem satisfies every positive-probe budget at once.
\end{proof}

The quantifier order matters. The cutoff $p_n$ here concerns a
single permutation satisfying every positive-probe bound.
The earlier cutoff $p_n/2$ concerns the union of the admissible
permutations as the probe varies. These classify different
information. Every statement above includes $n=0$, $p_n=2$,
the full prefix length $n+1$, and finite permutations; no odd-prime
or large-rank exception is needed.
