Green--Tao as an explicit external supply input; its complete Lean transport
construction remains separate.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{One residue class and the complete Dirichlet zero signal}

An intrinsic class-only observable has a different reference from the
full-prime transport above. There is a particularly useful choice: the
identity class $1\bmod q$, with a fixed integer $q\ge2$.
All Dirichlet characters take value one at that class.

\begin{theorem}[Positive character folding]\label{prop:identity-class-folding}
Initially for $\Re s>1$, put
\[
 G_q(s)=\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}\frac{\log p}{p^s}.
\]
Then
\[
 G_q(s)=-\frac1{\varphi(q)}
       \sum_{\chi\bmod q}\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)-H_q(s),
 \qquad
 H_q(s)=\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime},\ k\ge2\\p^k\equiv1\ (q)}}
                  \frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}.
\]
The remainder is holomorphic on $\Re s>1/2$. A zero $\rho$ with
$1/2<\Re\rho<1$ of any character in this family is a pole of $G_q$, with
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_{s=\rho}G_q(s)
   =-\frac1{\varphi(q)}\sum_{\chi\bmod q}m_\chi(\rho)\ne0.
\]
Here $m_\chi(\rho)=0$ when that character has no zero at $\rho$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the absolutely convergent Euler region, expand
\[
 -\frac{L'}L(s,\chi)
  =\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\p\nmid q}}\sum_{k\ge1}
          \chi(p)^k\frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}.
\]
Character orthogonality gives
$\varphi(q)^{-1}\sum_\chi\chi(p)^k
 ={\bf1}_{p^k\equiv1\ (q)}$.
Separate $k=1$ to obtain the asserted identity. Primes dividing $q$
contribute to neither side. For every fixed $\sigma_0>1/2$ and
$\Re s\ge\sigma_0$,
\[
 |H_q(s)|
 \le \frac1{1-2^{-\sigma_0}}
           \sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\log n}{n^{2\sigma_0}}<\infty.
\]
This modulus-independent majorant gives normal convergence and holomorphy.
Continue by the finite sum of logarithmic derivatives. At a zero in the
stated strip, each contributing logarithmic derivative has residue
$m_\chi(\rho)$; no $L$-function has a pole there. All multiplicities have
the same sign in their sum, while $H_q$ is holomorphic. The residue is
therefore nonzero, including when several characters share the zero.
\end{proof}

This identity has a direct probability interpretation. For each prime
$p\equiv1\bmod q$, take a Bernoulli variable $B_p$ of success probability
$1/p$, independently over each finite prefix. Its odds are precisely
$1/(p-1)$, as in the coefficient law. The weighted mean is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 M_q(x)=\mathbb E\!\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}(\log p)B_p
       =\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}\frac{\log p}{p},\\
 \int_{1^-}^{\infty}x^{-s}\,dM_q(x)=G_q(s+1)
 \qquad(\Re s>0).
 \end{gathered}
\]
Consequently the centered transform
\[
 \mathcal D_q(s)=G_q(s+1)-\frac1{\varphi(q)s}
\]
is holomorphic on $\Re s>-1/2$ if and only if GRH holds for the characters
modulo $q$ (equivalently, their associated primitive $L$-functions).
The subtraction removes the principal-character pole. Nonprincipal
characters are regular and nonzero at one; finite imprimitive Euler
factors introduce no zeros in $\Re s>1/2$.
The functional equations pair $\chi$ with $\overline\chi$, both present
in the family, so absence of right-half-strip zeros gives the full
critical-line assertion.

For an explicit sufficient arithmetic estimate, fix $1/2\le c<1$.
If there is a constant $C_q$ such that, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
 M_q(x)-\frac{\log x}{\varphi(q)}-C_q
       =O_{q,\varepsilon}(x^{c-1+\varepsilon}),
\]
then every nontrivial zero in this Dirichlet family has real part at most
$c$. Indeed, integration by parts gives, initially for $\Re s>0$,
\[
 \mathcal D_q(s)
  =C_q+s\int_1^\infty
     \left(M_q(x)-\frac{\log x}{\varphi(q)}-C_q\right)x^{-s-1}\,dx.
\]
The assumed estimate continues this expression holomorphically to
$\Re s>c-1$, contradicting the nonzero residue of any zero with
$\Re\rho>c$.

\begin{corollary}[Cyclotomic polynomial realization]
\label{cor:cyclotomic-class-observable}
Fix $q\ge2$ and put $F_q(u)=\Phi_q(u)$, the $q$th cyclotomic polynomial.
For a divisor prime $p\nmid q$, its local root count and probability odds are
\[
 \rho_q(p)=\varphi(q){\bf1}_{p\equiv1\ (q)},\qquad
 \eta_q(p)=\frac{\rho_q(p)}{p-\rho_q(p)}.
\]
For each fixed cutoff $N$, sample $u$ uniformly modulo
$\prod_{p\le N,\ p\nmid q}p$. The weighted distinct-prime-divisor count has mean
\[
 \mathbb E\!\sum_{\substack{p\le N,\ p\nmid q\\p\mid F_q(u)}}\log p
    =\varphi(q)M_q(N).
\]
Its Mellin--Stieltjes transform has residue
$-\sum_{\chi\bmod q}m_\chi(\rho)$ at $s=\rho-1$ for every zero
$1/2<\Re\rho<1$ in the Dirichlet family. All characters contribute with
the same sign.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $p\nmid q$, the polynomial $X^q-1$ is separable over $\mathbb F_p$.
The roots of $\Phi_q$ are exactly the elements of multiplicative order $q$.
The cyclic group $\mathbb F_p^\times$ has $\varphi(q)$ such elements when
$q\mid p-1$, and none otherwise. In the nonzero case $p\ge q+1>\varphi(q)$,
so the local success probability $\rho_q(p)/p$ is strictly below one.
The Chinese remainder theorem makes these finite-prime divisibility
events independent under the stated sampling law. Their odds give the
displayed coefficient law, and linearity of expectation gives its weighted
mean. The preceding character identity supplies the Mellin residue after
multiplication by $\varphi(q)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite cyclotomic sampling]
\label{thm:cyclotomic-finite-sampling}
Fix $q\ge2$ and a finite set $S$ of primes. Sample $U$ uniformly modulo
$M_S=\prod_{p\in S}p$, and define
\[
 B_p(U)={\bf1}_{p\nmid q,\ p\mid\Phi_q(U)},\qquad
 \theta_{q,p}=
 \begin{cases}
  \varphi(q)/p,&p\nmid q,\ p\equiv1\pmod q,\\
  0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
For every prescribed pattern $\varepsilon:S\to\{0,1\}$,
\[
 \Pr(B_p(U)=\varepsilon_p\text{ for all }p\in S)
 =
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=1}}\theta_{q,p}
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=0}}(1-\theta_{q,p}).
\]
All factors satisfy $0\le\theta_{q,p}<1$. In particular, every $p\le q$
has $\theta_{q,p}=0$. The statement includes $S=\varnothing$, with
$M_S=1$ and both products equal to one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Chinese remaindering bijects scalar residues modulo $M_S$ with tuples
$(u_p)_{p\in S}\in\prod_{p\in S}\mathbb F_p$. A success coordinate has
$\rho_q(p)$ permitted values, and a failure coordinate has
$p-\rho_q(p)$, where $\rho_q(p)=p\theta_{q,p}$ includes the depleted case.
The number of tuples giving the pattern is therefore exactly
\[
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=1}}\rho_q(p)
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=0}}(p-\rho_q(p)).
\]
Dividing by $M_S$ proves the formula. The preceding cyclotomic root
calculation gives the local probabilities. If a nondepleted success
is possible, $q\mid p-1$, so $q\le p-1$ and
$\varphi(q)\le q<p$. This also excludes every $p\le q$.
\end{proof}

This is an exact law for each fixed finite divisor support, without a
sampling-limit error. It asserts independence across divisor primes for
fixed $q$. Different cyclotomic families evaluated at the same scalar
need not be independent.

Thus polynomial inputs realize the intrinsic progression observable,
including quadratic and quartic examples:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi_3(u)&=u^2+u+1,& \Phi_4(u)&=u^2+1,\\
 \Phi_5(u)&=u^4+u^3+u^2+u+1,\\
 \Phi_8(u)&=u^4+1,& \Phi_{12}(u)&=u^4-u^2+1.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The class-$1\bmod5$ example includes characters of order four, beyond the
quadratic characters modulo eight. Here $u$ is the sampled polynomial
input and $N$ is the divisor cutoff. Primes dividing $q$ are explicitly
removed; no assertion about their root counts is used. For fixed $q$ they
form a finite exceptional set. The rescaled remainder is
$\varphi(q)H_q$, so its bound carries this factor if $q$ varies.
The local first-order term is character dependent; this argument does not
silently qualify the law as a pointwise quadratic perturbation of the
canonical prime law.

The finite-field root counts, their prime-modulus specialization, strict
local probabilities, the actual \texttt{PrimeClockLaw}, its general
coefficient comparison, and the full finite sampling law are checked in
Lean. The maintained owners, under the project namespace, are
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/FieldTheory/Finite/Cyclotomic.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicProbability.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicSampling.lean
\end{quote}
All nineteen declarations have only the standard foundational axioms
in their audits. The weighted expectation formula is an ordinary
consequence of the checked finite distribution and linearity of
expectation; it has no separately checked declaration here.
Character folding and the analytic continuation argument remain ordinary
written proofs, with no priority claim. They identify a concrete class-only
probability observable and an explicit remaining arithmetic estimate.
Existence of primes or progressions in the class does not itself provide
that estimate.

\subsection{An entire profile that remembers every prime}

Each proper nonzero fraction $\rho_p/p$ has a prime denominator in lowest
terms. This permits exact reconstruction at every prime, including inactive
primes. For the forward compensated Bernoulli product, see
\cite[Proposition~3.1]{KN}. Here the prime denominators supply an inverse
conclusion without a common leading asymptotic.

\begin{theorem}[Prime-labelled reconstruction from the zero set]
\label{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity}
For each prime $p$, let $a_p,b_p$ be integers with
$0\le a_p<p$ and $0\le b_p<p$, and suppose
\[
 \sum_p(a_p/p)^2<\infty,\qquad \sum_p(b_p/p)^2<\infty.
\]
The canonical probability-centred profiles
\[
 \mathcal P_a(z)=
 \prod_p\bigl(1+(a_p/p)(z-1)\bigr)
           \exp\bigl(-(a_p/p)(z-1)\bigr),
\]
and $\mathcal P_b$ are entire. Their zero sets recover every coordinate:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal Z(\mathcal P_a)=\mathcal Z(\mathcal P_b)
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\ a_p=b_p\quad\text{for every prime }p\\
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathcal P_a=\mathcal P_b.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Zero-set inclusion already implies $a_p=b_p$ wherever $a_p>0$.
Every zero is negative and simple.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On each compact set the compensated factor is
$1+O_R((a_p/p)^2)$, giving locally uniform convergence.
A sufficiently late tail has an absolutely convergent logarithm wherever
its factors are nonzero. Consequently the zeros are exactly
\[
 z=1-\frac{p}{a_p}\qquad(a_p>0).
\]
If a zero contributed at $p$ is also contributed at a prime $q$ by
$b_q>0$, then $p\,b_q=q\,a_p$. Hence $p\mid q\,a_p$.
Since $0<a_p<p$, we have $p\nmid a_p$, so $p\mid q$, whence $p=q$.
Cancellation gives $a_p=b_p$. This proves the inclusion assertion.

Apply this argument in both directions when the zero sets agree.
If $a_p=0<b_p$, reverse inclusion would force $b_p=a_p$.
Thus all coordinates agree, giving equality of the products as well.
Conversely, equality of the entire functions gives equality of their zeros.

No two positive coordinates of one profile can contribute the same zero.
At its unique vanishing factor, the remaining convergent product is
nonzero. The linear factor has nonzero derivative, so the zero is simple.
Finally $a_p<p$ implies $1-p/a_p<0$.
\end{proof}

The strict upper bound is essential: a deterministic success has factor
$z\exp(-(z-1))$, and moving it to another prime preserves the profile.
All probabilities may vanish, giving the constant profile one.
The theorem recovers local root counts; different polynomials may have
the same such counts.

\begin{corollary}[Complete cyclotomic local-law fingerprint]
\label{cor:cyclotomic-profile-fingerprint}
Fix $q\ge2$, use the depleted root counts $\rho_q(p)$ above, and put
$\theta_{q,p}=\rho_q(p)/p$. Then
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=
 \prod_p(1+\theta_{q,p}(z-1))
             \exp(-\theta_{q,p}(z-1))
\]
is entire, with simple zeros exactly at
\[
 \left\{\,1-\frac{p}{\varphi(q)}:
          p\text{ prime},\ p\equiv1\pmod q\,\right\}.
\]
For $q,r\ge2$, equality of these zero sets, equality of the entire
profiles, and $\rho_q(p)=\rho_r(p)$ at every prime are equivalent.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The explicit root count gives
\[
 0\le\theta_{q,p}\le\frac{\varphi(q)}p
       \le\frac{\varphi(q)}{p-1},\qquad \theta_{q,p}<1.
\]
The squared probabilities are therefore summable. Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity}.
At an active prime the numerator is precisely $\varphi(q)$;
$q\mid p-1$ already implies $p\nmid q$, giving the displayed zero set.
\end{proof}

For the actual scalar sampling experiment, the exact finite law gives
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
  \exp\!\left(-(z-1)\sum_{p\le N}\theta_{q,p}\right)
  \mathbb E_N z^{\sum_{p\le N}B_p(U_N)} .
\]
Each finite expectation uses its complete Chinese remainder period.
These profile zeros lie in the generating variable $z$. The Dirichlet
zeros in the preceding subsection occur in the Mellin continuation of
the corresponding weighted prime count.

A profile determines the local law rather than a unique modulus:
$q=3$ and $q=6$ give the same depleted counts, since the relevant primes
are odd. In contrast, the laws for $q=3$ and $q=4$ differ at $p=7$.
Both facts are consistent with exact every-prime reconstruction.

\begin{theorem}[Exact intersection of cyclotomic profile zero sets]
\label{thm:cyclotomic-profile-common-zeros}
Fix $q,r\ge2$, and let $L=\operatorname{lcm}(q,r)$.
If $\varphi(q)\ne\varphi(r)$, the zero sets of $\mathcal C_q$
and $\mathcal C_r$ are disjoint.
If $\varphi(q)=\varphi(r)=D$, their intersection is exactly
\[
 \mathcal Z(\mathcal C_q)\cap\mathcal Z(\mathcal C_r)
 =\left\{\,1-\frac pD:
                 p\text{ prime},\ p\equiv1\pmod L\,\right\}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For general proper prime-denominator laws, suppose a zero contributed
by $a_p>0$ is also contributed by $b_\ell>0$. Then
$p/a_p=\ell/b_\ell$, hence $p b_\ell=\ell a_p$.
As $p$ is prime and $0<a_p<p$, it follows that $p=\ell$ and
then $a_p=b_p$.
Conversely, an agreeing positive coordinate contributes the same zero
to both profiles. Thus a common zero is equivalent to an agreeing
positive count at the same prime.

At an active cyclotomic coordinate the counts are $\varphi(q)$ and
$\varphi(r)$. They must therefore be equal. When both equal $D$, a
prime contributes to both profiles exactly when
$q\mid p-1$ and $r\mid p-1$, or equivalently $L\mid p-1$.
Its zero is $1-p/D$. Every prime satisfying this condition contributes
to both, which proves the reverse inclusion as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact shared-support factorization]\label{thm:cyclotomic-shared-factor}
Let $q,r\ge2$, $L=\operatorname{lcm}(q,r)$ and $d_m=\varphi(m)$.
Define the residual entire function
\[
 R_{q\setminus r}(z)=
 \prod_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\q\mid p-1,\ r\nmid p-1}}
 \left(1+\frac{d_q}{p}(z-1)\right)
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{d_q}{p}(z-1)\right).
\]
Then, with the displayed compensation retained exactly,
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=
 \mathcal C_L\!\left(1+\frac{d_q}{d_L}(z-1)\right)
 R_{q\setminus r}(z).
\]
The analogous identity holds with $q,r$ interchanged. Moreover,
\[
 R_{q\setminus r}(z)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,p\ {\rm prime}:\quad
 q\mid p-1,\quad r\nmid p-1,\quad z=1-\frac{p}{d_q}.
\]
The two residual functions have disjoint zero sets.
If $d_q=d_r=D$, the common entire factor is
\[
 A_{q,r}(z)=
 \mathcal C_L\!\left(1+\frac{D}{\varphi(L)}(z-1)\right),
\]
and $\mathcal C_q=A_{q,r}R_{q\setminus r}$,
$\mathcal C_r=A_{q,r}R_{r\setminus q}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The prime support $q\mid p-1$ splits into $L\mid p-1$ and
$q\mid p-1,\ r\nmid p-1$. On the first part,
\[
 \eta_q(p)=\frac{d_q}{p}
         =\frac{d_q}{d_L}\,\eta_L(p).
\]
For $B_a(z)=(1+a(z-1))\exp(-a(z-1))$ the exact identity
$B_{ca}(z)=B_a(1+c(z-1))$ includes both the linear factor and
the exponential. Squared local probabilities are summable, so the
products of $B_a$ converge locally uniformly. Equivalently,
$\sum_p|B_{\eta_q(p)}(z)-1|$ converges for every fixed $z$.
Splitting this convergent product over the two complementary supports
therefore proves the factorization, including at its zeros.

The residuals inherit square summability, take local probabilities
in $[0,1)$, and are entire. The zero theorem for compensated Bernoulli
products gives the displayed residual zero set. If the two residuals
shared a zero, their full profiles would share it as well.
The preceding common-zero theorem forces $d_q=d_r$.
Their residual prime labels $p,t$ would then satisfy
$1-p/d_q=1-t/d_q$, hence $p=t$. One residual requires
$r\nmid p-1$, whereas the other requires $r\mid p-1$,
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The support-partition identity is proved for arbitrary square-summable
Bernoulli laws and arbitrary, possibly infinite supports. The scaling
identity itself needs no convergence hypothesis, since its local
factors agree pointwise. The actual cyclotomic factorization, residual
entireness and residual zero statements above are kernel checked;
no division by a profile at a zero is used.

The infinitude in these formulas is unconditional, by Dirichlet's
theorem in the identity class. More quantitatively, write
$\pi(X;m,1)$ for the number of primes at most $X$ that are $1$ modulo
$m$, and count profile zeros in $[-T,0]$. The exact formulas and the
prime number theorem in a fixed progression give
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_q(T)&=\pi(\varphi(q)(T+1);q,1)
                \sim \frac{T}{\log T},\\
 N_{q,r}(T)&=\pi(D(T+1);L,1)
                \sim \frac{D}{\varphi(L)}\,\frac{T}{\log T}
       \quad\bigl(\varphi(q)=\varphi(r)=D\bigr).
 \end{split}
\]
Here $q,r$ are fixed and $T\to\infty$; these prime-counting
consequences are ordinary deductions, not additional checked Lean
declarations. Every cyclotomic profile has the same leading zero
density, while the overlap records the intersection of progressions.
For example, $q=5$ and $r=8$ have $D=4$ and $L=40$, so their shared
zeros have relative density $1/4$. For $q=3,r=4$ it is $1/2$.
Different totients instead give no shared profile zeros.
These statements concern zeros in $z$, and introduce no new
Riemann or Dirichlet zero-free estimate.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform separation and stable prime-label recovery]
\label{thm:prime-profile-stable-recovery}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity},
suppose additionally that $a_p,b_p\le D$ for every prime, where
$D\ge1$ is an integer.
Any two distinct zeros $z,w$ drawn from the two profiles satisfy
\[
 |z-w|\ge \frac1{D^2}.
\]
More precisely, distinct proper prime/count labels $(p,r)$ and
$(\ell,s)$, with $1\le r<p$, $1\le s<\ell$ and $r,s\le D$,
satisfy
\[
 \left|\left(1-\frac p r\right)
           -\left(1-\frac\ell s\right)\right|
       \ge\frac1{rs}\ge\frac1{D^2}.
\]
Consequently, an observed complex location within strictly less than
$1/(2D^2)$ of a zero can match at most one such prime/count label.
The constants are independent of the height of the zero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The prime-label argument shows that $ps-\ell r$ is a nonzero integer
for distinct admissible labels. Its absolute value is at least one.
Since $r,s>0$, clearing denominators gives
\[
 \left|\left(1-\frac p r\right)
           -\left(1-\frac\ell s\right)\right|
   =\frac{|ps-\ell r|}{rs}\ge\frac1{rs}\ge\frac1{D^2}.
\]
Every profile zero has one of these labels by the exact zero formula,
which proves separation between any two distinct zeros, including
zeros taken from different profiles.
If an observation were within the stated strict tolerance of two
different labels, the triangle inequality would put their locations
strictly less than $1/D^2$ apart, contradicting the bound.
\end{proof}

For the actual progression profiles, take
$D=\max(\varphi(q),\varphi(r))$ for fixed $q,r\ge2$.
Then every distinct pair of zeros from $\mathcal C_q$ and
$\mathcal C_r$ has distance at least $D^{-2}$.
This is a uniform lower bound, with no optimality claim.
It concerns accuracy of zero locations: a bound on the error in
function values alone still needs a separate analytic estimate to
control the displacement of zeros. The determinant inequality used
in the proof is elementary; the conclusion here is its application
to exact and approximate prime-labelled profile recovery.

The reconstruction, support-partition and actual cyclotomic results have
thirty-three checked declarations in the maintained owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/\\
\hspace*{1em}BernoulliProfilePartition.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/BernoulliProfile.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicProfileRigidity.lean
\end{quote}
under the project namespace. Their audits contain only the standard
foundational axioms. The simple-zero assertion follows from the
zero-factor argument above and is not a separately named declaration in
this batch. All comparisons here use the displayed probability-centred
compensation.

\subsection{Special integers: reciprocal-divisor fingerprints}

Directly sampling a special sequence and sampling divisors of one of
its members are different experiments. The latter gives a single exact
probability model for every positive integer, without an infinitude
assumption on the class from which that integer is selected.
For $n\ge1$, choose $d\mid n$ with probability
\[
 {\mathbb P}_n(d)=
 \frac{d^{-1}}{\sum_{e\mid n}e^{-1}},\qquad
 P_n(z)={\mathbb E}_n z^{\omega(d)}.
\]
Here $\omega(d)$ counts distinct prime factors and $\omega(1)=0$.
The divisor involution $d\mapsto n/d$ gives
$\sum_{d\mid n}d^{-1}=\sigma(n)/n$, so $P_n(0)=n/\sigma(n)$.

\begin{theorem}[Complete prime-power recovery and unitary divisibility]
\label{thm:divisor-profile-unitary}
For positive integers $n,m$, the zero set of $P_n$ determines $n$.
More precisely, put
\[
 S_{p,a}=1+p+\cdots+p^{a-1},\qquad a\ge1.
\]
The zeros of $P_n$ are exactly the distinct simple numbers
\[
 -\frac{p^a}{S_{p,a}}\qquad(p^a\Vert n).
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \begin{split}
 Z(P_n)=Z(P_m)&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad n=m,\\
 Z(P_n)\subseteq Z(P_m)&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,k\ge1:\ m=nk,\ \gcd(n,k)=1.
 \end{split}
\]
For $n=1$ the zero set is empty. Both conclusions also hold for
the canonically compensated profiles
\[
 \mathcal D_n(z)=e^{-\mu_n(z-1)}P_n(z),\qquad
 \mu_n=\sum_{p^a\Vert n}\frac{S_{p,a}}{p^a+S_{p,a}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under reciprocal divisor weight the exponent choices at distinct
primes are independent, since both the support and the weight factor:
\[
 P_n(z)=
 \prod_{p^a\Vert n}
 \frac{1+z\sum_{j=1}^{a}p^{-j}}
      {1+\sum_{j=1}^{a}p^{-j}}
 =
 \prod_{p^a\Vert n}
 \frac{p^a+S_{p,a}z}{p^a+S_{p,a}}.
\]
Each linear factor has the displayed zero.
Since $S_{p,a}\equiv1\pmod p$, the fraction $p^a/S_{p,a}$
is reduced. Its positive numerator therefore identifies the prime
$p$ and the exponent $a$. In particular, distinct prime powers from
different prime coordinates cannot contribute the same zero.
The product has exactly one factor for each positive prime valuation,
so all its zeros are simple. Its zero set recovers every positive
valuation and hence the integer.

Inclusion of the zero sets says that every prime dividing $n$
occurs in $m$ with exactly the same exponent. This is equivalent to
$m=nk$ with no prime common to $n$ and $k$. Conversely, such a
coprime factorization retains precisely those prime-power factors
of $P_n$ in $P_m$. Finally the exponential compensation never
vanishes, so it changes neither zero set.
\end{proof}

The profile itself is a finite compensated Bernoulli product with
success probabilities $S_{p,a}/(p^a+S_{p,a})$.
The maintained Lean results prove its exact zero formula, recover
arbitrary square-summable prime-indexed exponent functions, discharge
summability for every integer factorization, and prove the two integer
equivalences for $\mathcal D_n$, including unitary divisibility.
Their transfer to $P_n$ uses the ordinary product identity above.
There are fourteen checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/BernoulliDivisorProfile.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/ReciprocalDivisorInteger.lean
\end{quote}
under the project namespace, with only standard foundational axioms.
The finite weighted-divisor sampling identity in the displayed product
is proved ordinarily here; it is not yet a separately named Lean
sampling adapter. No priority claim is made for this inverse statement.

This fingerprint retains substantially more information than
abundancy alone. For example, both $6$ and $28$ are perfect, so both
have $P_n(0)=1/2$, but their zero sets are respectively
$\{-2,-3\}$ and $\{-4/3,-7\}$.
Zero-set inclusion also differs from ordinary divisibility:
$2\mid4$, but the zero $-2$ of $P_2$ is absent from $P_4$,
whose zero is $-4/3$.

\paragraph{The classical RH criterion in the same model.}
Robin's criterion, together with its colossally abundant
counterexample reduction, gives
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 P_n(0)>\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log n}
 \quad\hbox{for every integer }n\ge5041.
\]
One may equivalently quantify over just the colossally abundant
integers $n\ge5041$, or over just the superabundant integers in that
range: a counterexample exists among the former if RH fails, and
they are contained in the latter. These are translations of the
classical criterion, not new independent estimates
\cite[Section~2 and equation~(1.2)]{Lagarias}.
The denominator is positive on the stated range. Compensation
must be undone before reading this probability:
$P_n(0)=e^{-\mu_n}\mathcal D_n(0)$, rather than
$\mathcal D_n(0)$ itself.

\paragraph{What the named classes contribute.}
The common divisor model above applies to every existing member of
each class. Its relation to direct sampling is as follows.
\begin{description}
\item[Sophie Germain primes.]
For primes $p$ with $2p+1$ prime, direct counts are
$\omega(p)=1$ and $\omega(p(2p+1))=2$.
They have no growing distinct-factor rank regime.
No infinitude assumption is made. The integer fingerprint is valid
for each such $p$ or product; conditioning a polynomial sieve family
on both outputs being prime needs its own sampling theorem.
\item[Fermat and Mersenne primes.]
Use the precise values $2^{2^j}+1$ with $j\ge0$, or $2^j-1$
with $j\ge2$, only when prime.
Their individual divisor profiles each have one zero, at the
negative prime itself. This neither assumes infinitely many such
primes nor transfers an index cutoff to a divisor cutoff.
Uniform residue sampling of a cyclotomic polynomial is a separate
experiment from evaluating it at the fixed base $2$.
\item[Even and odd perfect numbers.]
Every hypothetical or actual perfect integer has $P_n(0)=1/2$.
For even perfect numbers the Euclid--Euler form specifies the two
prime coordinates and their exponents; the full profile retains them.
For odd perfect numbers the statement is conditional on a witness,
without assuming one exists. At $n\ge5041$ the value $1/2$ already
satisfies Robin's probability inequality, so restricting that
inequality to perfect numbers supplies no stronger RH criterion.
\item[Superabundant and colossally abundant numbers.]
Under the strict record convention, superabundant integers are
exactly the strict lower records of $P_n(0)$.
For a fixed $\epsilon>0$, a colossally abundant optimizer minimizes
$n^\epsilon P_n(0)$; all maximizing ties in the usual non-strict
definition are retained. These classes lie in the actual extremal
regime of the RH criterion. Exact reconstruction does not estimate
their probability relative to the critical threshold.
\item[Fibonacci values.]
Take $F_0=0,F_1=1$ and values $F_j$ for $j\ge2$,
distinguishing all Fibonacci values from Fibonacci primes.
Each positive value has the exact divisor fingerprint.
Uniform index sampling instead gives local divisibility through
ranks of apparition; simultaneous events are controlled by the
least common multiple of those ranks, so independence requires
a separate justification. The index cutoff, the value $F_j$,
and a prime-divisor cutoff are distinct parameters.
\item[Primorials, factorials and highly composite numbers.]
They also fit the finite model. A primorial uses exponent one at
each supported prime, whereas factorials and the extremal classes
retain nontrivial exponents. Direct sampling of these sequences
can force every fixed small prime in the limit, so a finite rank
shift does not justify applying a positive-rank prime clock to
that direct measure.
\end{description}
The zero-set theorem is uniform over positive integers and requires
no class-specific existence theorem. An RH implication still needs
the appropriate independent inequality or a proved transfer of its
probability law; injective encoding alone supplies neither.

\subsection{Fibonacci carriers and the golden-ratio clock}
\label{sec:fibonacci-golden-carriers}

The Fibonacci sequence supplies an exact prime clock through its
\emph{indices}. Set $F_0=0,F_1=1$, and, for each odd prime $\ell$,
let $P_\ell$ be the least prime factor of $F_\ell$.
This exists because $F_\ell>1$ for $\ell\ge3$.
The sampled variable is the integer index $n$; the event is
$P_\ell\mid F_n$. A label cutoff $X$ means $\ell\le X$.
It is neither a bound on $P_\ell$ nor a bound on the value $F_n$.

\begin{theorem}[A prime-index carrier of a strongly divisible sequence]
\label{thm:strong-divisibility-carrier}
Suppose $a_1=1$ and
$\gcd(a_m,a_n)=a_{\gcd(m,n)}$ for a sequence of nonnegative integers.
If $\ell$ is prime and $c\ne1$ divides $a_\ell$, then
\[
 c\mid a_n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ell\mid n
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\]
In particular, for every odd prime $\ell$ and every prime divisor
$c$ of $F_\ell$ this equivalence holds with $a_n=F_n$.
The selected primes $P_\ell$ are pairwise distinct.
For every finite set $S$ of odd primes and arbitrary marks $z_\ell$,
\[
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   z_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}}
 =
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   z_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{\ell\mid n}}.
\]
The product convention means a factor $1$ at an unsuccessful event,
including when a mark is zero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $\ell\nmid n$, primality gives $\gcd(\ell,n)=1$.
A common divisor $c$ of $a_\ell$ and $a_n$ would then divide
$a_1=1$, contradicting $c\ne1$. If $\ell\mid n$, the gcd identity
instead gives $\gcd(a_\ell,a_n)=a_\ell$, so
$a_\ell\mid a_n$ and hence $c\mid a_n$.
For Fibonacci numbers use their strong divisibility identity
$\gcd(F_m,F_n)=F_{\gcd(m,n)}$.
If $P_\ell=P_q$ at two prime labels, the equivalence at $n=q$
gives $\ell\mid q$, hence $\ell=q$.
Finally, each individual indicator in the two products is equal.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The real closed form and its alternating correction.}
Write
\[
 \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2},\qquad
 \psi=\frac{1-\sqrt5}{2}=-\varphi^{-1}.
\]
Binet's formula and its exact relative error are
\begin{align*}
 F_n&=\frac{\varphi^n-\psi^n}{\sqrt5}
     =\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}
       \bigl(1-(-1)^n\varphi^{-2n}\bigr),\\
 F_n-\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}
     &=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}\varphi^{-n}}{\sqrt5}.
\end{align*}
Both powers satisfy the Fibonacci recurrence, and their difference
divided by $\varphi-\psi=\sqrt5$ has initial values $0,1$;
this proves the formula. Its absolute error is at most
$1/\sqrt5<1/2$, so $F_n$ is the nearest integer to
$\varphi^n/\sqrt5$ for every $n\ge0$.
The sign alternates with parity and the relative error is
$\varphi^{-2n}$. This real approximation explains the exponential
value scale. The divisibility clock uses the same quadratic equation
over finite fields, as follows.

\begin{theorem}[Golden-root order and Fibonacci divisibility]
\label{thm:fibonacci-golden-clock}
For roots $u,v$ of $T^2-T-1$ in any commutative ring,
\[
 (u-v)F_n=u^n-v^n.
\]
In a field, if $v=1-u$ and $u\ne v$, then
\[
 F_n=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \left(\frac{u}{v}\right)^n=1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (-u^2)^n=1.
\]
Here $F_n$ is interpreted by the natural-number map into the field.

For an odd prime $r\ne5$, choose
$u=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ in $\mathbb F_{r^2}$ and put
$\chi_5(r)=(5/r)$. The Fibonacci entry point
$\mathfrak z(r)=\min\{n\ge1:r\mid F_n\}$ satisfies
\[
 \mathfrak z(r)=\operatorname{ord}(-u^2),
 \qquad
 \mathfrak z(r)\mid r-\chi_5(r).
\]
The exceptional primes have the exact rules
\[
 2\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow3\mid n,\qquad
 5\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow5\mid n.
\]
Thus $\mathfrak z(2)=3$, $\mathfrak z(5)=5$,
$P_3=2$ and $P_5=5$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The ring identity follows from the initial values at $n=0,1$
and the recurrence: $u^2=u+1$ and $v^2=v+1$ ensure that both
sides satisfy the same second-order recursion.
For $v=1-u$, these identities give $uv=-1$, so $v\ne0$
and $u/v=-u^2$. With $u-v\ne0$, the ring identity can be
divided by $u-v$ and $v^n$, proving the power test.

For $r\ne2,5$, the discriminant $5$ is nonzero and the two roots
in $\mathbb F_{r^2}$ are distinct. If $\chi_5(r)=1$, both roots
belong to $\mathbb F_r$, so their nonzero quotient has order
dividing $r-1$. If $\chi_5(r)=-1$, the Frobenius automorphism
interchanges the two roots. For $t=u/v$ this gives
$t^r=v/u=t^{-1}$, hence $t^{r+1}=1$.
In either case the least positive exponent giving $t^n=1$
is exactly the least index with $r\mid F_n$.

Modulo $2$, the successive pairs in the Fibonacci recurrence
cycle through $(0,1),(1,1),(1,0)$, yielding precisely the indices
divisible by $3$. Modulo $5$ the roots coalesce at $3$; direct
substitution in the recurrence gives
$F_n=n\,3^{n-1}$ for $n\ge1$, with $n=0$ handled separately.
Since $3$ is invertible, vanishing is equivalent to $5\mid n$.
The selected carriers follow from $F_3=2$ and $F_5=5$.
\end{proof}

The use of finite fields is through the integral quadratic
identity, not a reduction map from the real field.
For the selected carrier and any odd prime label $\ell\ne5$,
the appropriate distinct-root field therefore gives
$\operatorname{ord}(-u^2)=\ell$.
At $P_3=2$ one may use a root of $T^2+T+1$ in $\mathbb F_4$;
its quotient has order $3$.
At $P_5=5$ the repeated-root formula supplies the clock instead.
This explicitly identifies the two exceptional labels.

For $\ell>5$ the carrier is odd and different from $5$.
The order bound consequently gives
\[
 \ell\mid P_\ell-\chi_5(P_\ell),\qquad
 2\ell-1\le P_\ell\le F_\ell<\varphi^\ell.
\]
Indeed, $P_\ell\pm1$ is even, so its positive multiple of the
odd prime $\ell$ is a multiple of $2\ell$.
The upper bound uses $P_\ell\mid F_\ell$ and Binet's formula.
These inequalities connect the real growth scale and the modular
clock while keeping label order and carrier magnitude distinct.

\begin{theorem}[The marked Fibonacci law and an inherited RH criterion]
\label{thm:fibonacci-marked-nicolas}
For a finite set $S$ of odd primes put $M_S=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell$.
Uniform index sampling over $0\le n<M_S$ gives, for every binary
pattern $b:S\to\{0,1\}$,
\[
 \mathbb P\!\left(
   \boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}=b_\ell
   \text{ for all }\ell\in S\right)
 =
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   \left(\frac1\ell\right)^{b_\ell}
   \left(1-\frac1\ell\right)^{1-b_\ell}.
\]
The empty support is allowed, with period $1$.
For $N\ge1$ and sampling over $1\le n\le N$, the probability of any fixed
pattern differs from this product by at most $M_S/N$.

For real $X\ge3$ let $S_X$ be the odd primes at most $X$,
$M_X=M_{S_X}$, and
\[
 A(X)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
   \#\{1\le n\le N:
       P_\ell\nmid F_n\ \hbox{for every }\ell\in S_X\}.
\]
Then
\[
 A(X)=\prod_{\ell\in S_X}(1-1/\ell),
\]
and the classical Nicolas criterion gives the exact equivalence
\[
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A(X)<\frac{2e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log(2M_X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The carrier theorem replaces every Fibonacci event by $\ell\mid n$.
The Chinese remainder theorem identifies a uniform residue modulo
$M_S$ with independent uniform residues modulo the distinct labels.
There is one successful residue at each label and $\ell-1$
unsuccessful residues, proving every pattern probability.
For arbitrary $N=kM_S+t$, $0\le t<M_S$, there are $k$ complete
periods and one remainder. If $a$ residues in a full period give
the pattern, its total count is $ka+r$ with $0\le r\le t$.
The difference from $Na/M_S$ is $r-ta/M_S$, of absolute value
at most $t<M_S$. This proves the stated error and the fixed-cutoff
limit. Divisibility at $F_0=0$ is well-defined; this sampling
argument does not apply a reciprocal-divisor measure to zero.

For $X\ge3$, the full primorial is $2M_X$, so
$\theta(X)=\log(2M_X)>1$ and
\[
 f(X)=e^\gamma\log\theta(X)
          \prod_{p\le X}(1-1/p)
      =\frac{e^\gamma}{2}\log\log(2M_X)\,A(X).
\]
Every factor outside $A(X)$ is positive. Thus the displayed
probability inequality is exactly $f(X)<1$, or $F(X)<0$,
with $f,F$ as in \eqref{eq:analytic-objects}.
The Nicolas equivalence already used above is precisely
eventual $F(X)<0$ \cite{Nicolas1983,Nicolas2012}.
\end{proof}

The order of limits is essential: $S_X$ is fixed while
$N\to\infty$, and only afterward does $X\to\infty$.
The explicit sampling error $M_X/N$ gives no unproved uniform
growing-cutoff assertion. The finite generating polynomial is
\[
 G_X(z)=\prod_{\ell\in S_X}(1+(z-1)/\ell).
\]
After canonical compensation the profiles
\[
 \mathcal F_X(z)=
 \prod_{\ell\in S_X}
   (1+(z-1)/\ell)e^{-(z-1)/\ell}
\]
converge locally uniformly to an entire function, since
$\sum_\ell\ell^{-2}<\infty$. The existing Bernoulli profile
zero theorem gives zeros exactly $1-\ell$ at the odd prime labels.
These zeros encode index labels; the naturally ordered,
unmarked prime factors of $F_n$ form a different observable.

The maintained candidate owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/StrongDivisibilityCarrier.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FibonacciGoldenCarrier.lean
\end{quote}
check twenty-two declarations: the generic carrier and its exact
finite count/product transport, the ring Binet identity, the
field power tests, prime-index orders, and the two exceptional
integer clocks. Six more checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Helpers/FibonacciProbability.lean
\end{quote}
reuse the existing CRT equivalence to identify the actual marked
events and prove their full finite-period joint law.
All twenty-eight axiom audits contain only the standard
foundational axioms. The finite-field existence and Frobenius
classification, interval remainder estimate, and displayed
Nicolas equivalence are ordinary proofs here.
Entry-point arithmetic and finite-field torus descriptions are
classical; see Cubre--Rouse \cite{CubreRouse}.
The contribution here is their exact use as a marked probability
carrier and the explicit transfer to this existing RH criterion.
No infinitude of Fibonacci primes is assumed, and no improved
zero-free exponent follows without a new independent estimate.

\subsection{Nested Fermat carriers and a joint Fibonacci profile}
\label{sec:fermat-fibonacci-joint}

Fermat numbers need not be prime to supply exact clocks. Write
$T_j=2^{2^j}+1$ for $j\ge0$ and choose its least prime factor $R_j$.
Here the sampled value is $2^n-1$, with scalar exponent $n\ge0$:
we test $R_j\mid2^n-1$. This is a different observable from
testing divisibility directly on the Fermat sequence $T_n$.
The cutoff $k$ selects the labels $0\le j<k$, independently of
the sizes of $R_j$ and $2^n-1$.

\begin{theorem}[Fermat divisors give a nested dyadic clock]
\label{thm:fermat-dyadic-clock}
If $r>2$ divides $T_j$, then
\[
 \operatorname{ord}_{r}(2)=2^{j+1},\qquad
 r\mid2^n-1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad2^{j+1}\mid n.
\]
In particular all prime factors of a fixed $T_j$ have exactly the
same event on this sampled sequence. The selected primes $R_j$
are pairwise distinct, and $R_0=3$.
For
\[
 C_k(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{R_j\mid2^n-1}
       =\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{2^{j+1}\mid n},
\]
uniform sampling over $0\le n<2^k$ gives
\[
 \mathbb P(C_k=t)=2^{-t-1}\quad(0\le t<k),\qquad
 \mathbb P(C_k=k)=2^{-k}.
\]
Thus, with $G_k(z)=\mathbb E z^{C_k}$,
\begin{align}
 G_k(z)&=\frac12\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}(z/2)^t+(z/2)^k,
 \label{eq:fermat-geometric-pgf}\\
 (2-z)G_k(z)&=1+(1-z)(z/2)^k,\qquad
 \mathbb E C_k=1-2^{-k}.
 \label{eq:fermat-geometric-compensation}
\end{align}
The cross-multiplied identity holds for every complex $z$,
including $z=2$, where $G_k(2)=1+k/2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In $\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z$ the hypothesis gives
$2^{2^j}=-1\ne1$, while $2^{2^{j+1}}=1$.
In particular $2$ is a unit. Its order divides $2^{j+1}$ but
does not divide $2^j$, so the order is exactly $2^{j+1}$.
The power test proves the claimed divisibility equivalence,
including $n=0$. A shared prime factor at two Fermat labels
would consequently have two different exact orders, which is
impossible. The first selected factor is the prime $T_0=3$.

For $t<k$, exactly $2^{k-t-1}$ residues in a complete period
are divisible by $2^t$ and not by $2^{t+1}$.
Their count $C_k$ is $t$. Only the zero residue is divisible by
$2^k$, and it has count $k$. This proves the distribution and
\eqref{eq:fermat-geometric-pgf}, also when $k=0$.
The indicator definition handles the zero residue without assigning
a finite $2$-adic valuation to zero. Multiplying the finite
geometric sum proves the cross identity without division by $2-z$.
Alternatively, splitting the sampled indices into even and odd
parts gives $G_0=1$ and $G_{k+1}=(1+zG_k)/2$.
Finally, summing the threshold probabilities gives
$\mathbb E C_k=\sum_{j<k}2^{-j-1}=1-2^{-k}$.
\end{proof}

These events are nested, not independent Bernoulli trials.
Nor is an infinitude of Fermat primes assumed: $R_j$ exists
even when $T_j$ is composite. Direct sampling of $T_n$ would
give a different law: each fixed $R_j$ divides only $T_j$,
so its density among Fermat indices is zero.

\begin{theorem}[A common-index product law and its exact compensation]
\label{thm:fermat-fibonacci-profile}
Let $S$ be a finite set of odd prime labels,
$M_S=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell$, and $k\ge0$.
Sample the same index $n$ uniformly over
$0\le n<2^kM_S$, evaluating the pair $(F_n,2^n-1)$.
For every binary Fibonacci pattern $b$ and every complex $z$,
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 z^{C_k(n)}
 \boldsymbol{1}_{
   \boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}=b_\ell\ \text{for every }\ell\in S}\right]
 =
 G_k(z)\prod_{\ell\in S}
 \left(\frac1\ell\right)^{b_\ell}
 \left(1-\frac1\ell\right)^{1-b_\ell}.
\]
Consequently, for arbitrary complex marks $w_\ell$,
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 z^{C_k(n)}
 \prod_{\ell\in S}w_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}}\right]
 =G_k(z)\prod_{\ell\in S}(1+(w_\ell-1)/\ell).
\]
The entire nested Fermat block is independent of the Fibonacci
block. No independence is asserted within the Fermat block.

For $S_X$ the odd primes at most $X$, put
$\mu_X=\sum_{\ell\in S_X}1/\ell$ and let
\[
 T_{X,k}(n)=C_k(n)+
   \sum_{\ell\in S_X}\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}.
\]
Compensation by the \emph{actual mean} gives
\[
 H_{X,k}(z)
 =e^{-(z-1)(\mu_X+1-2^{-k})}\,
   \mathbb E z^{T_{X,k}}.
\]
As $X,k\to\infty$, independently, these functions converge
locally uniformly on $|z|<2$ to
\[
 H(z)=\frac{e^{1-z}\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)}{2-z},
 \qquad
 \mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)=
 \prod_{\ell\ge3\ {\rm prime}}
   (1+(z-1)/\ell)e^{-(z-1)/\ell}.
\]
This limit extends meromorphically to the whole plane.
It has a simple pole at $2$, with residue
$-e^{-1}\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(2)\ne0$.
The exact compensation
\[
 (2-z)e^{z-1}H(z)=\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)
 \label{eq:fermat-fibonacci-pole-removal}
\]
extends to an entire function and recovers all the simple zeros
$1-\ell$, for odd primes $\ell$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The previous carrier theorems replace the events by
$2^{j+1}\mid n$ and $\ell\mid n$, respectively.
Since $2^k$ and $M_S$ are coprime, CRT identifies one uniform
scalar residue with independent uniform residues modulo $2^k$
and modulo $M_S$. The first coordinate determines the whole
Fermat count; the second determines the full Fibonacci pattern.
This proves the first formula. Summing over all binary patterns,
with the stated marks, gives the second formula and the mean
$\mu_X+1-2^{-k}$. Empty supports and $k=0$ are included.

The exact finite identity gives, for $z\ne2$,
\[
 G_k(z)-\frac1{2-z}
 =\frac{(1-z)(z/2)^k}{2-z}.
\]
For every fixed $0\le R<2$ this implies the uniform bound
\[
 \sup_{|z|\le R}\left|G_k(z)-\frac1{2-z}\right|
 \le \frac{1+R}{2-R}(R/2)^k.
\]
The dyadic mean correction tends uniformly on compact sets to
$e^{1-z}$. The previously proved canonical Bernoulli products
converge locally uniformly to $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}$.
Their product therefore gives the stated two-parameter limit.

The displayed quotient supplies the meromorphic extension.
Every factor at $z=2$ is positive, and the logarithms of the
factors have an absolutely convergent sum, because their
absolute values are bounded by a constant times $\ell^{-2}$.
Thus $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(2)>0$ and the residue is nonzero.
Multiplying by the stated entire compensator removes this pole.
Away from $2$ the removed factors are nonzero, so all odd-prime
profile zeros and their multiplicities are preserved.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{A shared physical prime does not mean a shared index event.}
The same prime $5$ occurs as $P_5=5$ and $R_1=5$, yet
\[
 5\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow5\mid n,\qquad
 5\mid2^n-1\Longleftrightarrow4\mid n.
\]
These two events have probabilities $1/5$ and $1/4$ and joint
probability $1/20$ under uniform sampling modulo $20$.
Their independence comes from their coprime index clocks.
Likewise $P_3=2$ supplies the index-$3$ event, while $R_0=3$
supplies the index-$2$ event. Thus the arithmetic names of the
carrier primes are not the parameters of the probability law.

\paragraph{The RH information and the order of limits.}
For $k\ge1$ the event $C_k=0$ is exactly $n$ odd.
Writing $A(X)$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:fibonacci-marked-nicolas},
the limiting density of avoiding every selected carrier in both
sampled sequences is therefore
\[
 B(X)=\frac12 A(X)=\prod_{p\le X}(1-1/p).
\]
The existing Nicolas criterion becomes
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 B(X)<\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log(2M_X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X\ge3.
\]
The extra nested Fermat events beyond $R_0$ do not change this
vacancy event. This exact translation supplies no new independent
bound on the product or on zeta zeros.

All finite probability formulas above first use a complete period.
For fixed $X,k$, interval sampling over $1\le n\le N$, $N\ge1$,
has pattern error at most $2^kM_X/N$, by the same period-remainder
argument as before. If a fixed finite weight has absolute value
at most $W$, the corresponding expectation error is at most
$2^{k+1}M_XW/N$: the incomplete period and its predicted average
each have absolute contribution at most $2^kM_XW$ before
dividing by $N$.
Only after $N\to\infty$ at fixed cutoffs are $X,k$ allowed to grow.
The local uniform profile limit does not assert simultaneous
growing-cutoff sampling accuracy.

\paragraph{Formalization scope.}
The maintained candidate owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatCarrier.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatCarrierProbability.lean
\end{quote}
check twenty-nine declarations: exact orders and integer power
tests, distinct selected carriers, actual dyadic sample sums and
their geometric recurrence, compensation at all $z$, the
exceptional value at $2$, and the exact mean.
Four further checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Helpers/FermatFibonacciProbability.lean
\end{quote}
prove coprimality of the sample periods, pointwise compatibility
of the actual scalar events with CRT, the generating-sum
factorization over arbitrary commutative semirings, and the
normalized real joint law. All thirty-three axiom audits contain
only the standard foundational axioms. The complex normalized
law follows by the same finite algebra; the interval estimate,
locally uniform limit, residue, and RH translation above are
ordinary proofs, with no separate Lean compilation claim.
The elementary order and geometric-law mechanisms are classical.
The purpose of this construction is
their common-index realization alongside the Fibonacci golden-root
clock and the exact preservation of the marked profile.


\subsection{Rational residue-class laws: a stronger inverse theorem}

The preceding inverse theorem extends beyond a common leading coefficient.
Fix an integer $Q\ge1$. Suppose that outside finitely many primes the laws
have the form $\eta_1(p)=R_a(p)$ and $\eta_2(p)=S_a(p)$ for
$p\equiv a\pmod Q$, where $a$ runs over the units modulo $Q$.
Each $R_a,S_a$ is a rational function with rational coefficients.
Assume each is either identically zero or has an expansion
$\nu/x+\kappa/x^2+O(x^{-3})$ with rational $\nu>0$.
The actual probabilities are in $[0,1]$.

\begin{theorem}[Residue-rational profile rigidity]
Equality of the entire profiles implies $R_a=S_a$ as rational functions
for every unit class $a$. The complete laws then differ by a finite
permutation of prime coordinates.
More precisely, if one active $R_a$ differs from $S_a$, there is a unit
progression $r\pmod M$, refining $a\pmod Q$, such that for every
sufficiently large prime $p\equiv r\pmod M$ the value $R_a(p)$ is absent
from the entire second law.
Consequently the first profile has infinitely many zeros absent from
the second. Using the prime number theorem in a fixed progression,
their number in $[-T,0]$ is at least
$(\nu/\varphi(M)+o(1))T/\log T$.
If only the second law is active in a discrepant class, interchange
the two laws.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $R_a(x)=\nu/x+\kappa/x^2+O(x^{-3})$.
For each active target class $b$, write
$S_b(x)=\mu_b/x+\lambda_b/x^2+O(x^{-3})$.
If $R_a(p)=S_b(q)$ and $p$ is large, then $q$ is large and comparable
to $p$: finitely many small target primes have probabilities bounded
away from the positive values tending to zero under consideration.
Taking reciprocals gives
\[
 q=\frac{\mu_b}{\nu}p+
       \left(\frac{\lambda_b}{\mu_b}
                    -\frac{\mu_b\kappa}{\nu^2}\right)+O(p^{-1}).
\]
Both displayed coefficients are rational. The error belongs to a
fixed rational lattice because $p,q$ are integers, so it is eventually
zero. Thus every large equality satisfies an exact affine equation
$C_bq=A_bp+B_b$, with positive integers $A_b,C_b$ and integer $B_b$.

If $B_b=0$, primeness and sufficiently large $p,q$ force $q=p$ and
$A_b=C_b$. Then $b=a$, and $R_a(p)=S_a(p)$ has only finitely many
solutions unless the two rational functions are identical.
For each remaining equation, $B_b\ne0$. Choose a distinct auxiliary
prime $\ell_b$ not dividing $QA_bB_bC_b$, and impose
$p\equiv-B_bA_b^{-1}\pmod{\ell_b}$.
This is a nonzero residue. Together with $p\equiv a\pmod Q$, the
Chinese remainder theorem gives one unit class modulo
$M=Q\prod_b\ell_b$. Each affine equality on this class would make
$\ell_b\mid q$, impossible once the prime $q>\ell_b$.
This excludes every target class simultaneously; the finite
exceptional target primes were already removed.

Dirichlet's theorem supplies infinitely many primes in the chosen
unit progression. Their probabilities are eventually distinct,
since $R_a(p)^{-1}=p/\nu-\kappa/\nu^2+O(p^{-1})$ is strictly
increasing on large prime arguments. They therefore give distinct
exclusive zeros. Their cutoff is $p\le\nu T+O(1)$, which yields the
stated count from the fixed-progression prime number theorem.
Equal profiles cannot have such exclusive zeros. Hence all rational
functions agree, the labelled tails agree, and cancellation of the
common tail gives the finite-permutation conclusion.
\end{proof}

This is an ordinary proof; its finite affine-exclusion and
residue-rational classification have not yet been formalized in Lean.
It applies only after a family's rational local laws and exceptional
prime set have been established. It does not assert such a transfer
for every polynomial, marked family, or Dirichlet $L$-function.

\subsection{The completed signed-packet calculus and its open consumer}

For a finite $S\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}$ and complex coefficients $a,b$,
put $A_d=\sum_{n\in S,\ d\mid n}a_n$ and
$B_d=\sum_{n\in S,\ d\mid n}b_n$.
The maintained signed covariance identity is exactly
\[
 \sum_{\substack{n,m\in S\\(n,m)=1}}
      (a_n-cb_n)\overline{(a_m-cb_m)}
    =\sum_{d=1}^{N}\mu(d)|A_d-cB_d|^2.
\]
Indeed $\sum_{d\mid(n,m)}\mu(d)$ is the coprimality indicator, and
finite interchange of sums proves the identity. Each divisor row
retains the full expansion
\[
 |A_d-cB_d|^2=|A_d|^2
    -cB_d\overline{A_d}-\overline c A_d\overline{B_d}
    +|c|^2|B_d|^2.
\]
In particular, if $b$ is supported on primes larger than $D$, then
$B_d=0$ for every $2\le d\le D$. Such a prime-only compensator changes
none of these low rows, regardless of the complex coefficient $c$.
This rules out that specific compensation mechanism on those rows;
it is not an obstruction to other coupled compensators.

There is also an exact partition using every prime allowed by the
finite support, rather than a chosen finite set. For $v_1=0$, define,
for $1\le n\le N$ and prime $p\le N$,
\[
 v_p(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\mu(n)v_n\frac{\log p}{\log n},&p\mid n,\ p\ {\rm prime},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Only $n>1$ enters the first branch. Since $\mu(n)=0$ off the
squarefree numbers, $\sum_{p\le N}v_p(n)=\mu(n)v_n$ for
$1\le n\le N$. The coprime form of two blocks with the same prime
index vanishes exactly: both supported arguments are divisible by
that prime. Writing $\mathcal C(f,g)=\sum_{n,m\in S,\,(n,m)=1}f_n\overline{g_m}$,
this gives the exact identity
\[
 \mathcal C(\mu v,\mu v)=
 \sum_{\substack{p,q\le N\\p,q\ {\rm prime},\ p\ne q}}
            \mathcal C(v_p,v_q).
\]
The coefficient $v$ may itself retain a full compensation, so this
identity does not erase its cross terms. It does not estimate their
joint cancellation.

The reconciliation inventory
\path{paper/completed-research-inventory.json}
and the public theorem-audit inventory record the maintained owners
for these covariance identities, full-prime partitions, Gaussian
principal and Poisson inputs, Euler-continuation results, detector
adapters, and quantitative tail lemmas.
Their public export is
\path{PrimeFactorOscillations/Assembly/CompletedDetectorPackets.lean}.
A copied source is not certified by this list: the inventory separately
records the fresh compilation and axiom-audit status.
An earlier 20 October reconciliation checked 1,139 owned Lean modules and
4,843 selected theorem closures with standard foundations only; both the
public and canonical QRH statement comparisons passed. That reconciliation
identified 301 maintained owners and transferred all 132 proved private
graph nodes to maintained sources. A later 1,150-module promotion covered
the identity, finite-permutation and prime-coordinate inverse consumers.
Subsequent maintained owners add the full adaptive cutoff inverse and
exact optimal-distortion theorem, with separate build and axiom receipts.
The residue-rational classification and continuum-cardinality assertion
remain ordinary proofs. The signed arithmetic estimate for an improved
RH boundary remains open.

The unfinished analytic consumer remains explicitly open. In the
fixed-mask notation of that programme it asks, for each
$\varepsilon>0$ and each fixed admissible $G$, for constants
$C_{\varepsilon,G},D_{0,\varepsilon,G}$ such that
\[
 |I_{D,G}|\le C_{\varepsilon,G}D^{3/2+\varepsilon}
 \quad(D\ge D_{0,\varepsilon,G}).
\]
No uniformity for a growing $G$ is implicit. The identities above do
not prove this estimate. Detector precision, zero-signal preservation
and smaller separate remainders are recorded as structural results;
the certified unconditional boundary remains $7/8$.
Failed absolute-value reductions and restricted-range attempts retain
their failure records and are not counted as improvements of that boundary.
